In recent times, a deluge of curates has descended upon the north of England, blanketing the hills in such density that every parish boasts one or more. They are young, vigorous, and theoretically destined to perform great deeds of charity. Yet, we shall not speak of this modern era. We turn our gaze backward to the beginning of the nineteenth century. The present age is dusty, sunburnt, and arid; we shall evade its harsh noon, retreat into a siesta, and dream only of the dawn.

Do not let this prelude mislead you into expecting a romance. Do not anticipate sentiment, poetry, or reverie. Do not look for passion, stimulation, or melodrama. Calm your expectations; lower them to a humble standard. Before you lies something real, cool, and solid—something as unromantic as a Monday morning when the working class wakes with the grim realization that they must rise and labor. It is not denied that you might later taste excitement, perhaps near the meal's conclusion, but the first dish served shall be austere enough for a Catholic, even an Anglo-Catholic, to consume on Good Friday during Passion Week: cold lentils and vinegar without oil, unleavened bread with bitter herbs, and no roast lamb.

In those distant years, the abundant shower of curates had not yet fallen. In 1811–1812, curates were scarce. There was no Pastoral Aid, no Additional Curates' Society to assist worn-out rectors in hiring vigorous young colleagues from Oxford or Cambridge. The successors of the apostles, disciples of Dr. Pusey and instruments of the Propaganda, were then merely being hatched under cradle-blankets or undergoing regeneration via nursery-baptism in wash-hand basins. One could not guess, gazing at any of them, that the Italian-ironed double frills of their net caps concealed the brows of preordained saints, nor foresee in the folds of their long night-gowns the white surplices they would later wield to exercise the souls of parishioners and confound old-fashioned vicars.

Yet, even in those days of scarcity, the precious plant existed, though rare. A favored district in the West Riding of Yorkshire could boast three rods of Aaron blossoming within a twenty-mile radius. You shall see them. Step into this neat garden-house on the skirts of Whinbury, walk into the little parlour. There they are at dinner. Allow me to introduce them: Mr. Donne, curate of Whinbury; Mr. Malone, curate of Briarfield; Mr. Sweeting, curate of Nunnely. These are Mr. Donne's lodgings, belonging to John Gale, a small clothier. Mr. Donne has kindly invited his brethren to dine with him. You and I shall join the party to observe and listen. For now, however, they are merely eating; while they eat, we shall converse aside.

These gentlemen are in the prime of youth, possessing the boundless energy of that age—an energy their moping old vicars wish to channel into pastoral duties, urging them to supervise schools and visit the sick. But the youthful Levites find this dull. They prefer lavishing their energies on a routine that, to others, appears heavy with ennui and cursed with monotony, yet yields them an unfailing supply of enjoyment and occupation.

I refer to their frantic back-and-forth movement between their respective lodgings—not a circle, but a triangle of visits maintained year-round, regardless of season or weather. With unintelligible zeal, they brave snow, hail, wind, rain, mire, and dust to dine, drink tea, or sup with one another. What attracts them is difficult to define. It is not friendship, for they quarrel whenever they meet. It is not religion; the topic is never named, though theology is occasionally discussed, piety is never mentioned. Nor is it the love of food and drink; each could enjoy equally good joints, puddings, and tea at his own lodging. Mrs. Gale, Mrs. Hogg, and Mrs. Whipp—their respective landladies—assert that "it is just for naught else but to give folk trouble." By "folk," the good ladies mean themselves, for indeed they are kept in a perpetual "fry" by this system of mutual invasion.

Mr. Donne and his guests are at dinner; Mrs. Gale waits on them, though a spark of the hot kitchen fire burns in her eye. She considers the privilege of hosting friends occasionally, without extra charge—a perk included in her letting terms—to have been exercised sufficiently recently. This week is only Thursday. On Monday, Mr. Malone of Briarfield came to breakfast and stayed for dinner. On Tuesday, Mr. Malone and Mr. Sweeting of Nunnely came to tea, stayed for supper, occupied the spare bed, and visited her again at breakfast on Wednesday. Now, on Thursday, they are both here for dinner, and she is certain they will stay all night. "C'en est trop," she would say if she could speak French.

Mr. Sweeting minces his slice of roast beef, complaining of its toughness; Mr. Donne remarks that the beer is flat. Indeed, that is the worst of it: if they were merely civil, Mrs. Gale would not mind so much. If they seemed satisfied with what they received, she would not care. But "these young parsons are so high and scornful, setting everyone beneath their 'fit.'" They treat her with less than civility precisely because she does not keep a servant but performs the household work herself, as her mother did before her. Then they constantly speak against Yorkshire ways and Yorkshire folk; by that very token, Mrs. Gale believes none of them to be a true gentleman or of gentle kin. "The old parsons are worth the whole lump of college lads; they know what belongs to good manners and are kind to high and low."

"More bread!" cries Mr. Malone, in a tone that, though prolonged for only two syllables, proclaims him a native of the land of shamrocks and potatoes. Mrs. Gale hates Mr. Malone more than either of the others, yet she fears him. He is a tall, strongly built figure with genuine Irish legs and arms, and a face that is authentically national—not the Milesian type, nor Daniel O'Connell's style, but the high-featured, North-American-Indian visage belonging to a certain class of Irish gentry. It has a petrified, proud look, better suited to the owner of a slave estate than to the landlord of a free peasantry. Mr. Malone's father called himself a gentleman; he was poor, in debt, and besottedly arrogant, and his son was like him.

Mrs. Gale offered the loaf.

"Cut it, woman," said her guest; and the "woman" cut it accordingly. Had she followed her inclinations, she would have cut the parson as well; her Yorkshire soul revolted absolutely from his manner of command.

The curates had good appetites. Though the beef was "tough," they ate a great deal of it. They swallowed a tolerable allowance of the "flat beer," while a Yorkshire pudding and two tureens of vegetables disappeared like leaves before locusts. The cheese received distinguished attention, and a "spice-cake," served as dessert, vanished like a vision. Its elegy was chanted in the kitchen by Abraham, Mrs. Gale's son and heir, a youth of six summers. He had counted on the reversion of the cake, and when his mother brought down the empty platter, he lifted his voice and wept sorely.

Meanwhile, the curates sat sipping their wine, a liquor of unpretending vintage, moderately enjoyed. Mr. Malone, indeed, would have preferred whisky, but Mr. Donne, being an Englishman, did not keep the beverage. While sipping, they argued—not on politics, philosophy, or literature, topics now entirely without interest for them—not even on practical or doctrinal theology, but on minute points of ecclesiastical discipline, frivolities that seemed empty as bubbles to all save themselves. Mr. Malone, contriving to secure two glasses of wine while his brethren contented themselves with one, gradually became hilarious in his fashion: insolent, speaking rudely in a hectoring tone, and laughing clamorously at his own brilliance.

Each companion became his butt in turn. Malone had a stock of jokes he served regularly on such occasions, seldom varying his wit, for he never considered himself monotonous and cared little for others' opinions. He favored Mr. Donne with hints about his extreme meagreness, allusions to his turned-up nose, cutting sarcasms regarding a threadbare chocolate surtout worn whenever it rained, and criticisms of a choice set of Cockney phrases and pronunciations, Mr. Donne's own property and certainly deserving remark for the elegance they communicated to his style.

Mr. Sweeting was bantered regarding his stature—he was a little man, a mere boy in height and breadth compared to the athletic Malone; rallied on his musical accomplishments—he played the flute and sang hymns like a seraph, some young ladies of his parish thought; sneered at as "the ladies' pet"; and teased about his mother and sisters, for whom poor Mr. Sweeting retained a lingering regard, and of whom he was foolish enough to speak in the presence of the priestly Paddy, from whose anatomy the bowels of natural affection had somehow been omitted.

The victims met these attacks in their own ways: Mr. Donne with stilted self-complacency and half-sullen phlegm, the sole props of his otherwise somewhat rickety dignity; Mr. Sweeting with the indifference of a light, easy disposition that never professed to have any dignity to maintain.

When Malone's raillery became too offensive, which it soon did, they joined forces to turn the tables, asking him how many boys had shouted "Irish Peter!" after him that day as he walked the road (Malone's name was Peter—the Rev. Peter Augustus Malone); requesting to know if it was the Irish custom for clergymen to carry loaded pistols in their pockets and a shillelah in their hands during pastoral visits; inquiring the meaning of words like *vele*, *firrum*, *hellum*, *storrum* (which Mr. Malone invariably pronounced *veil*, *firm*, *helm*, *storm*); and employing other retaliatory methods suggested by their innate refinement.

This, of course, would not do. Malone, neither good-natured nor phlegmatic, was presently in a towering passion. He vociferated and gesticulated; Donne and Sweeting laughed. He reviled them as Saxons and snobs at the very top pitch of his high Celtic voice; they taunted him with being a native of a conquered land. He threatened rebellion in the name of his "counthry," venting bitter hatred against English rule; they spoke of rags, beggary, and pestilence. The little parlour was in uproar; one might have thought a duel must follow such virulent abuse. It seemed a wonder that Mr. and Mrs. Gale did not take alarm at the noise and summon a constable to keep the peace. But they were accustomed to such demonstrations. They well knew that the curates never dined or took tea together without a little exercise of the sort, and were quite easy regarding consequences, knowing that these clerical quarrels were as harmless as they were noisy, resulting in nothing, and that, on whatever terms the curates parted that night, they would surely meet as the best of friends the next morning.

As the worthy pair sat by their kitchen fire, listening to the repeated, sonorous contact of Malone's fist with the mahogany plane of the parlour table, the consequent start and jingle of decanters and glasses following each assault, the mocking laughter of the allied English disputants, and the stuttering declamation of the isolated Hibernian, a foot was heard on the outer doorstep, and the knocker quivered to a sharp appeal.

Mr. Gale went to open the door.

"Whom have you upstairs in the parlour?" asked a voice—remarkable in tone, nasal and abrupt.

"O Mr. Helstone, is it you, sir? I could hardly see you for the darkness; it is so soon dark now. Will you walk in, sir?"

"I want to know first whether it is worth my while walking in. Whom have you upstairs?"

"The curates, sir."

"What! All of them?"

"Yes, sir."

"Been dining here?"

"Yes, sir."

"That will do."

With these words, a person entered—a middle-aged man dressed in black. He walked straight across the kitchen to an inner door, opened it, inclined his head forward, and stood listening. There was something to listen to, for the noise above was just then louder than ever.

"Hey!" he ejaculated to himself, then turning to Mr. Gale—"Have you often this sort of work?"

Mr. Gale had been a churchwarden and was indulgent to the clergy.

"They're young, you know, sir—they're young," he said deprecatingly.

"Young! They want caning. Bad boys—bad boys! And if you were a Dissenter, John Gale, instead of being a good Churchman, they'd do the like—they'd expose themselves; but I'll----"

To finish this sentence, he passed through the inner door, drew it after him, and mounted the stairs. After listening for a few minutes upon arrival at the upper room, he made entrance without warning and stood before the curates.

They were silent; they were transfixed; and so was the invader. He—a personage short of stature but straight of port, bearing on broad shoulders a hawk's head, beak, and eye, the whole surmounted by a Rehoboam or shovel hat, which he did not seem to think necessary to lift or remove before the presence in which he stood—folded his arms on his chest and surveyed his young friends, if friends they were, much at his leisure.

"What!" he began, delivering his words in a voice no longer nasal but deep—more than deep, a voice made purposely hollow and cavernous—"What! Has the miracle of Pentecost been renewed? Have the cloven tongues come down again? Where are they? The sound filled the whole house

The night was a suffocating blanket of gray rain, swallowing the stars and moon until the sky itself seemed a heavy, sable curtain. Malone, a man who rarely paused to read the subtle poetry of nature, trudged along the causeway, his Irish habit of wearing his hat backward adding a touch of casual defiance to his weary stride. He saw only the destination: the distant, muffled glow of the Redhouse inn, where the warmth of a fire and the clinking of glasses promised a tumbler of whisky-and-water. Yet, knowing the patrons were Mr. Helstone's own parishioners who knew him too well, he sighed and pressed on, leaving the comfort behind.

Leaving the highroad, he cut across the monotonous fields toward Hollow's Mill, a shortcut that required leaping hedges and walls. In the darkness, he spotted a solitary light glowing in the valley below—a beacon he steered toward. It led him to a small white house, its color stark against the gloom. A fresh-faced servant admitted him, revealing a narrow passage and stairs trimmed with crimson baize and carpet, a splash of vibrant cleanliness in the damp air.

"Mr. Moore is at home?" Malone asked.

"Yes, sir, but he is not in," the servant replied. "He is at the mill, in the counting-house."

Guided by the voice of a woman—Sarah, it seemed, with a curl of paper securing her hair against the wind—Malone hurried toward the massive black structure of the mill. The workday was done; the machinery slept. Malone found a chink of light in the sooty flank of the building and knocked, the thick end of his shillelah ringing out a clear tattoo.

A key turned, and the door opened.

"Is it Joe Scott? What news of the wagons?"

"No; it's myself. Mr. Helstone would send me."

"Oh! Mr. Malone." The voice held a faint note of disappointment. "I regret extremely Mr. Helstone should have thought it necessary to trouble you so far. There was no necessity—I told him so—and certainly not on such a night. But walk forwards."

Inside, the room was a stark contrast to the outside world. It was bright, warmed by an excellent fire and illuminated by an elegant lamp, yet the furniture was plain: stiff-backed green chairs, a solid desk, and walls adorned with plans for machinery and gardens. Malone, relieved from the damp chill, hung his wet coat and settled into a chair by the hearth.

"Comfortable quarters you have here, Mr. Moore," he said, warming his knees near the red grate.

"Yes, but my sister would be glad to see you, if you prefer stepping into the house."

"Oh no! The ladies are best alone. I never was a lady's man. You don't mistake me for my friend Sweeting, do you, Mr. Moore?"

"Sweeting! Which of them is that? The gentleman in the chocolate overcoat, or the little gentleman?"

"The little one—the cavalier of the Misses Sykes, with the whole six of whom he is in love, ha! ha!"

"Better be generally in love with all than specially with one, I should think, in that quarter."

"But he is specially in love with one besides," Malone pressed. "Which do you think?"

With a quiet smile, Mr. Moore replied, "Dora, of course, or Harriet."

"Ha! ha! You've an excellent guess. But what made you hit on those two?"

"Because they are the tallest, the handsomest, and Dora, at least, is the stoutest. And as your friend Mr. Sweeting is but a little slight figure, I concluded that, according to a frequent rule in such cases, he preferred his contrast."

"You are right; Dora it is. But he has no chance, has he, Moore?"

"What has Mr. Sweeting besides his curacy?" Malone asked, laughing for three minutes before answering. "Why, David has his harp, or flute, which comes to the same thing. He has a sort of pinchbeck watch; ditto, ring; ditto, eyeglass. That's what he has."

"How would he propose to keep Miss Sykes in gowns only?"

"Ha! ha! Excellent! I'll ask him that next time I see him. I'll roast him for his presumption. But no doubt he expects old Christopher Sykes would do something handsome. He is rich, is he not? They live in a large house."

"Sykes carries on an extensive concern."

"Therefore he must be wealthy, eh?"

"Therefore he must have plenty to do with his wealth, and in these times would be about as likely to think of drawing money from the business to give dowries to his daughters as I should be to dream of pulling down the cottage there, and constructing on its ruins a house as large as Fieldhead."

"Do you know what I heard, Moore, the other day?"

"No. Perhaps that I _was_ about to effect some such change. Your Briarfield gossips are capable of saying that or sillier things."

"That you were going to take Fieldhead on a lease, and that it was your intention to settle a Miss Sykes there as mistress—to be married, in short, ha! ha! Now, which is it? Dora, I am sure. You said she was the handsomest."

"I wonder how often it has been settled that I was to be married since I came to Briarfield. They have assigned me every marriageable single woman by turns in the district. Now it was the two Misses Wynns—first the dark, then the light one; now the red-haired Miss Armitage; then the mature Ann Pearson. At present you throw on my shoulders all the tribe of the Misses Sykes. On what grounds this gossip rests God knows. I visit nowhere; I seek female society about as assiduously as you do, Mr. Malone. If ever I go to Whinbury, it is only to give Sykes or Pearson a call in their counting-house, where our discussions run on other topics than matrimony, and our thoughts are occupied with other things than courtships, establishments, dowries. The cloth we can't sell, the hands we can't employ, the mills we can't run, the perverse course of events generally, which we cannot alter, fill our hearts, I take it, pretty well at present, to the tolerably complete exclusion of such figments as love-making, etc."

"I go along with you completely, Moore. If there is one notion I hate more than another, it is that of marriage—I mean marriage in the vulgar weak sense, as a mere matter of sentiment—two beggarly fools agreeing to unite their indigence by some fantastic tie of feeling. Humbug! But an advantageous connection, such as can be formed in consonance with dignity of views and permanency of solid interests, is not so bad—eh?"

"No," responded Moore, in an absent manner. The subject seemed to have no interest for him; he did not pursue it. After sitting for some time gazing at the fire with a preoccupied air, he suddenly turned his head.

"Hark!" said he. "Did you hear wheels?"

Rising, he went to the window, opened it, and listened. He soon closed it. "It is only the sound of the wind rising," he remarked, "and the rivulet a little swollen, rushing down the hollow. I expected those wagons at six; it is near nine now."

"Seriously, do you suppose that the putting up of this new machinery will bring you into danger?" inquired Malone. "Helstone seems to think it will."

"I only wish the machines—the frames—were safe here, and lodged within the walls of this mill. Once put up, I defy the frame-breakers. Let them only pay me a visit and take the consequences. My mill is my castle."

"One despises such low scoundrels," observed Malone, in a profound vein of reflection. "I almost wish a party would call upon you to-night; but the road seemed extremely quiet as I came along. I saw nothing astir."

"You came by the Redhouse?"

"Yes."

"There would be nothing on that road. It is in the direction of Stilbro' the risk lies."

"And you think there is risk?"

"What these fellows have done to others they may do to me. There is only this difference: most of the manufacturers seem paralyzed when they are attacked. Sykes, for instance, when his dressing-shop was set on fire and burned to the ground, when the cloth was torn from his tenters and left in shreds in the field, took no steps to discover or punish the miscreants: he gave up as tamely as a rabbit under the jaws of a ferret. Now I, if I know myself, should stand by my trade, my mill, and my machinery."

"Helstone says these three are your gods; that the 'Orders in Council' are with you another name for the seven deadly sins; that Castlereagh is your Antichrist, and the war-party his legions."

"Yes; I abhor all these things because they ruin me. They stand in my way. I cannot get on. I cannot execute my plans because of them. I see myself baffled at every turn by their untoward effects."

"But you are rich and thriving, Moore?"

"I am very rich in cloth I cannot sell. You should step into my warehouse yonder, and observe how it is piled to the roof with pieces. Roakes and Pearson are in the same condition. America used to be their market, but the Orders in Council have cut that off."

Malone did not seem prepared to carry on briskly a conversation of this sort. He began to knock the heels of his boots together, and to yawn.

"And then to think," continued Mr. Moore, who seemed too much taken up with the current of his own thoughts to note the symptoms of his guest's ennui—"to think that these ridiculous gossips of Whinbury and Briarfield will keep pestering one about being married! As if there was nothing to be done in life but to 'pay attention,' as they say, to some young lady, and then to go to church with her, and then to start on a bridal tour, and then to run through a round of visits, and then, I suppose, to be 'having a family.' Oh, que le diable emporte!" He broke off the aspiration into which he was launching with a certain energy, and added, more calmly, "I believe women talk and think only of these things, and they naturally fancy men's minds similarly occupied."

"Of course—of course," assented Malone; "but never mind them." And he whistled, looked impatiently round, and seemed to feel a great want of something. This time Moore caught and, it appeared, comprehended his demonstrations.

"Mr. Malone," said he, "you must require refreshment after your wet walk. I forget hospitality."

"Not at all," rejoined Malone; but he looked as if the right nail was at last hit on the head, nevertheless. Moore rose and opened a cupboard.

"It is my fancy," said he, "to have every convenience within myself, and not to be dependent on the feminity in the cottage yonder for every mouthful I eat or every drop I drink. I often spend the evening and sup here alone, and sleep with Joe Scott in the mill. Sometimes I am my own watchman. I require little sleep, and it pleases me on a fine night to wander for an hour or two with my musket about the hollow. Mr. Malone, can you cook a mutton chop?"

"Try me. I've done it hundreds of times at college."

"There's a dishful, then, and there's the gridiron. Turn them quickly. You know the secret of keeping the juices in?"

"Never fear me; you shall see. Hand a knife and fork, please."

The curate turned up his coat-cuffs, and applied himself to the cookery with vigor. The manufacturer placed on the table plates, a loaf of bread, a black bottle, and two tumblers. He then produced a small copper kettle—still from the same well-stored recess, his cupboard—filled it with water from a large stone jar in a corner, set it on the fire beside the hissing gridiron, got lemons, sugar, and a small china punch-bowl; but while he was brewing the punch a tap at the door called him away.

"Is it you, Sarah?"

"Yes, sir. Will you come to supper, please, sir?"

"No; I shall not be in to-night; I shall sleep in the mill. So lock the doors, and tell your mistress to go to bed."

He returned.

"You have your household in proper order," observed Malone approvingly, as, with his fine face ruddy as the embers over which he bent, he assiduously turned the mutton chops. "You are not under petticoat government, like poor Sweeting, a man—whew! how the fat spits! it has burnt my hand—destined to be ruled by women. Now you and I, Moore—there's a fine brown one for you, and full of gravy—you and I will have no gray mares in our stables when we marry."

"I don't know; I never think about it. If the gray mare is handsome and tractable, why not?"

"The chops are done. Is the punch brewed?"

"There is a glassful. Taste it. When Joe Scott and his minions return they shall have a share of this, provided they bring home the frames intact."

Malone waxed very exultant over the supper. He laughed aloud at trifles, made bad jokes and applauded them himself, and, in short, grew unmeaningly noisy. His host, on the contrary, remained quiet as before.

It is time, reader, that you should have some idea of the appearance of this same host. I must endeavour to sketch him as he sits at table.

He is what you would probably call, at first view, rather a strange-looking man; for he is thin, dark, sallow, very foreign of aspect, with shadowy hair carelessly streaking his forehead

There is a peculiar alchemy in human spirits, a strange capacity for joy that defies the logic of circumstance. One might assume that a drizzling night, a perilous trek across the moors, and the grim business of hunting down lawbreakers would dampen the mood of any sensible man. Yet, as Mr. Helstone and Mr. Moore emerged from the mill-yard gates, leading their small retinue, they moved with a buoyancy that seemed entirely at odds with the weather. Lantern light flickered across their faces, illuminating a spark of lively defiance in Moore's dark eyes and a radiant, almost manic gleam in the rector's usually stern visage. To an observer from Stilbro' Moor, this pair would have been fair game for a shot from behind a hedge; they were walking straight into danger with the exhilaration of men who knew exactly what they were doing.

It is a well-known truth that a clergyman should be a man of peace, a gentle shepherd of souls, not a warrior clad in black. Yet, Mr. Helstone was neither diabolical nor devoid of principle. He was simply a man who had missed his true calling; he was born for the battlefield, but bound by duty to the pulpit. He was a creature of iron will, hard-handed and unyielding, a man of rigid conscience who could be cruel in his adherence to truth. He was loved by some and hated by many, a figure of such stark contrast that he seemed to embody the very friction of the age.

For his part, Mr. Moore was a man of equally hard lines, though of a different texture. A Whig by political necessity and a foreigner by birth, he possessed a bitterness that Helstone found both irritating and strangely compelling. As they rode side by side, the air between them crackled with the static of opposing ideologies. The war was the sole topic that united them, even as it drove them apart. Helstone, a high Tory, viewed the conflict as a sacred crusade; Moore, a man of pragmatic opposition, saw it as a futile struggle against an inevitable tide.

"The Peninsular news still holds your faith, I trust?" Moore asked, his voice cutting through the damp air.

Helstone's response was curt. "What do you mean?"

"Faith in the wooden-faced idol of England? Do you truly believe Wellington can send fire from heaven to consume the French holocaust?"

"I believe Wellington will flog Bonaparte's marshals into the sea whenever he chooses to lift his arm."

Moore scoffed, the sound sharp in the cold night. "My dear sir, you speak as if you are serious. Bonaparte commands an omnipotent spirit; your Wellington is a mere martinet, a dull-witted drover acting for a farmer no less dull. Against such genius, your 'good cause'—the restoration of a disgraced king—is a pathetic farce."

Helstone's temper flared. "Against legitimacy stands usurpation! Against modest, righteous resistance stands boastful, treacherous ambition! God defend the right!"

"God often defends the powerful," Moore countered calmly.

Helstone seized the biblical imagery with the fervor of a preacher. He spoke of the Israelites crossing the Red Sea, of the weak against the mighty, of the rod of Moses triumphing over the chariots of Pharaoh. "Crime and the lost archangel generalled the ranks of Pharaoh," he declared, his voice rising above the rain. "Which triumphed? We know the answer. The Lord saved Israel that day. The right hand of the Lord dashed in pieces the enemy!"

Moore listened, his expression unreadable, before offering his own twist on the metaphor. "France is Israel, and Napoleon is Moses. Europe, with her rotten dynasties, is corrupt Egypt. Gallant France is the Twelve Tribes, and her vigorous Usurper is the Shepherd of Horeb."

Helstone recoiled, unable to find a retort to such audacity. Moore pressed on, his voice dropping to a conspiratorial whisper. "In Italy, he was a true Moses, a regenerator of nations. It puzzles me how the conqueror of Lodi could condescend to become an emperor—a vulgar humbug—and how a people who once dreamed of liberty could sink back into slavery. I despise France! If England had marched as far as France, she would not have retreated so shamelessly."

"You imply that imperial France is no worse than republican France?" Helstone demanded fiercely.

"I mean to say nothing," Moore replied smoothly, "but I can think what I please. About revolutions, regicides, the divine right of kings, and the sanity of war..."

His sentence was abruptly cut short by the thunderous roll of wheels and the sudden halt of a gig in the middle of the road. Both men had been so engrossed in their philosophical duel that they had failed to hear the approach until it was upon them.

"Nah, maister; did th' wagons hit home?" a voice called out from the vehicle.

"Can that be Joe Scott?" Helstone asked.

"Ay, ay!" confirmed a second voice. Inside the gig sat two figures, illuminated by a single lantern. "Ay, Mr. Moore, it's Joe Scott. I'm bringing him back in a bonny pickle. I found him on the top of the moor, him and three others. What will you give me for restoring him to you?"

"Why, my thanks, I believe," Moore replied graciously. "I could better have afforded to lose a better man. That is you, I suppose, Mr. Yorke, by your voice?"

"Ay, lad, it's me," came the reply. "I was coming home from Stilbro' market, and just as I got to the middle of the moor... I heard a groan. I pulled up. Some would have whipped on faster, but I've naught to fear. I don't believe there's a lad in these parts who would harm me. I asked what was wrong, and Joe said they were lying in a ditch, teed with a bit of bandage. So I got down, loosened 'em with my penknife, and Scott rides with me to tell me all how it happened."

"Well, I am greatly obliged to you, Mr. Yorke," Helstone said stiffly.

"Are you, my lad? You know you're not," Yorke laughed, a rough, hearty sound. "However, here are the rest approaching. And look, by the Lord, another set with lights in their pitchers, like the army of Gideon! And as we've the parson with us—" he turned to Helstone with a grin—"good-evening, Mr. Helstone. We'll do."

Helstone returned the greeting with the rigidity of a statue. Yorke, however, was in a jovial mood. "We're eleven strong men, and there's both horses and chariots among us. If we could only fall in with some of these starved ragamuffins of frame-breakers, we could win a grand victory. We could be every one a Wellington—that would please you, Mr. Helstone—and such paragraphs as we could contrive for the papers! Briarfield should be famous. But we'll have a column and a half in the *Stilbro' Courier* over this job, I dare say. I expect no less."

"And I'll promise you no less, Mr. Yorke," Helstone replied, his tone sharpening. "I'll write the article myself."

"To be sure—sartainly! And mind ye recommend weel that them 'at brake t' bits o' frames, and teed Joe Scott's legs wi' band, should be hung without benefit of clergy. It's a hanging matter, or should be. No doubt of that."

"If I judged them, I'd give them short shrift!" cried Moore. "But I mean to let them alone this bout, to give them rope enough, certain that in the end they will hang themselves."

"Let them alone, will ye, Moore? Do you promise that?"

"Promise! No. All I mean to say is, I shall give myself no particular trouble to catch them; but if one falls in my way..."

"You'll snap him up, of course. Only you would rather they would do something worse than merely stop a wagon before you reckon with them."

"Well, we'll say no more on the subject at present," Yorke interrupted, steering his horse toward a distant light. "Here we are at my door, gentlemen. I hope you and the men will step in. You will none of you be the worse of a little refreshment."

Both Moore and Helstone initially opposed the invitation, citing the propriety of their mission and the inclemency of the night. Yet, the courtesy of the offer, combined with the inviting glow of the muslin-curtained windows, proved irresistible. They yielded, and Mr. Yorke, alighting from his gig which he left in charge of a servant, led the way into the house.

It was immediately apparent that Mr. Yorke was a man of complex contradictions. He shifted effortlessly between broad Yorkshire dialect and pure, educated English; his manner oscillated between bluntness and affability. His station was difficult to gauge by his speech alone, but the appearance of his residence clarified the mystery.

The men were directed to the kitchen, where Yorke promised they would be served "wi' summat to taste." The gentlemen were ushered into a magnificent hall lined with pictures, then conducted to a large parlour dominated by a roaring fire. It was a room of exceptional taste, devoid of ostentatious wealth but rich in the refined sensibilities of a traveler and a scholar. The walls were adorned with Italian views—genuine works of art depicting sunny pastoral scenes, with soft distances and blue air quivering between the hills. A guitar rested on a sofa, alongside beautiful miniatures and Grecian vases. Books were arranged with elegance in two tall cases.

Yorke bade his guests sit and rang for wine. He gave hospitable orders for the refreshment of the men in the kitchen, but the rector remained standing, refusing the wine offered to him.

"E'en as you will," Yorke remarked, his tone lightening. "I reckon you're thinking of Eastern customs, Mr. Helstone, and you'll not eat nor drink under my roof, feared we should be forced to be friends. But I am not so particular or superstitious. You might sup the contents of that decanter, and you might give me a bottle of the best in your own cellar, and I'd hold myself free to oppose you at every turn still—in every vestry-meeting and justice-meeting where we encounter one another."

"It is just what I should expect of you, Mr. Yorke," Helstone replied.

"Does it agree with you now, Mr. Helstone, to be riding out after rioters, of a wet night, at your age?"

"It always agrees with me to be doing my duty; and in this case, my duty is a thorough pleasure. To hunt down vermin is a noble occupation, fit for an archbishop."

"Fit for you, at any rate. But where's the curate? He's gone to visit some poor body in a sick bed, or he's hunting down vermin in another direction."

"He is doing garrison-duty at Hollow's Mill."

"You left him a sup of wine, I hope, Bob," Yorke turned to Moore, "to keep his courage up?"

He did not wait for an answer but continued, addressing Moore who had lazily claimed a chair by the fireside. "Move it, Robert! Get up, my lad! That place is mine. Take the sofa, or three other chairs, if you will, but not this. It belongs to me, and nobody else."

"Why are you so particular to that chair, Mr. Yorke?" Moore asked, vacating the seat in obedience.

"My father was before me, and that's all the answer I shall give thee; and it's as good a reason as Mr. Helstone can give for the main feck of his notions."

"Moore, are you ready to go?" Helstone inquired.

"Nay; Robert's not ready, or rather, I'm not ready to part with him. He's an ill lad, and wants correcting."

"Why, sir? What have I done?"

"Made thyself enemies on every hand."

"What do I care for that? What difference does it make to me whether your Yorkshire louts hate me or like me?"

"Ay, there it is. The lad is a make of an alien among us. His father would never have talked in that way.—Go back to Antwerp, where you were born and bred, mauvaise tête!"

"Mauvaise tête vous-même; je ne fais que mon devoir; quant à vos lourdauds de paysans, je m'en moque!"

"En revanche, mon garçon, nos lourdauds de paysans se moqueront de toi; sois en certain," Yorke replied, speaking with a French accent nearly as pure as Gérard Moore's.

"C'est bon! c'est bon! Et puisque cela m'est égal, que mes amis ne s'en inquiètent pas."

"Tes amis! Où sont-ils, tes amis?"

"Je fais écho, où sont-ils? et je suis fort aise que l'écho seul y réponde. Au diable les amis! Je me souviens encore du moment où mon père et mes oncles Gérard appellèrent autour d'eux leurs amis, et Dieu sait si les amis se sont empressés d'accourir à leur secours! Tenez, M. Yorke, ce mot, ami, m'irrite trop; ne m'en parlez plus."

"Comme tu voudras."

And here Mr. Yorke held his peace. Leaning back in his three-cornered carved oak chair, he watched the scene unfold, a silent observer in a room filled with the tension of clashing worlds.

Mr. Yorke was the very essence of a Yorkshire gentleman, a man of fifty-five who looked older, his hair a stark, silver-white. His face was broad rather than high, fresh and hale, yet carved with the harshness of the north that echoed in his voice. Every line of his countenance was thoroughly English—strikingly devoid of any Norman lineage. It was an inelegant, unclassic visage, lacking aristocratic polish; fine folk might have called it vulgar, but sensible ones would recognize it as characteristic. To the shrewd, it was a map of pith, sagacity, and rude, real originality. Yet, it was also the face of a man indocile, scornful, and sarcastic—a man difficult to lead and impossible to drive. Tall and wiry, with a stately integrity in his posture, he bore no trace of the clown.

To sketch his person was easy; to define his mind was far harder. Do not expect a benevolent philanthropist here. While he could speak with sense and good feeling to Mr. Moore, do not assume he always thought or spoke justly.

First, Mr. Yorke lacked the organ of veneration. This deficiency threw him askew wherever reverence was due. Kings, nobles, priests, dynasties, and parliaments were to him mere rubbish, an abomination. He found no pleasure in their forms or rights, believing the world would be clearer if their high places were razed and their occupants crushed. This lack of awe dried up the electric delight of admiration, withering a thousand pure sources of enjoyment. He was not irreligious—he believed in God and heaven—but his deity was one stripped of imagination, tenderness, and awe.

Secondly, he lacked the organ of comparison. This stripped him of sympathy, rendering him inconsistent. While professing doctrines of mutual toleration, he harbored a bigoted antipathy toward certain classes. He spoke of "parsons" and "lords" with a harshness and insolence that was unjust and insufferable. Unable to place himself in their shoes or weigh their temptations against their defects, he expressed ferocious wishes regarding those he deemed tyrannical. By his own threats, he would have employed cruel means to advance freedom. Equality was a word he spoke, yet at heart he was a proud man, haughty as Beelzebub toward anyone he deemed superior. Revolt ran in his blood; he could not bear control, nor could his ancestors or descendants.

His lack of general benevolence made him impatient of imbecility and faults that grated on his shrewd nature, leaving no check to his cutting sarcasm. He was not merciful; he would wound repeatedly, caring little for the depth of his thrusts.

As for his lack of ideality, it was hardly a fault. He possessed a fine ear for music and a correct eye for color, granting him taste. Who cares for imagination? Many deem it a dangerous, senseless attribute, akin to frenzy—a disease rather than a gift. To hear the fanatics speak, one believes their hearts are cold without that elixir, their eyes dim without that flame. It is an illusion, yet they cling to it for gold. Since Mr. Yorke possessed no poetic imagination, he considered it superfluous in others. He could tolerate painters and musicians because he relished their results, but a quiet poet, burning with fire yet unable to play the counting-house game, would live despised and die scorned under Hiram Yorke's gaze.

Yet, the true poet, though quiet externally, often harbors a truculent spirit and shrewdness beneath his placidity. He can measure the stature of those who look down on him and maintain a festal brightness in his bosom, independent of the world's cold judgment. He is not to be pitied; he laughs in his sleeve at misguided sympathizers. These, however, were not Mr. Yorke's reflections.

Mr. Yorke was one of the most honorable and capable men in Yorkshire, beloved by the poor for his fatherly kindness. To his workmen, he was considerate. When dismissing them, he tried to find them new employment or aid their removal. If insubordination arose, he crushed it in the germ, eradicating it like a bad weed. Secure in his own affairs, he felt free to speak severely of others, blaming their misfortunes entirely on their own faults while advocating for the operatives.

His family was the oldest in the district, and though not the wealthiest, he was highly influential. Educated well and having traveled the Continent before the French Revolution, he was adept in French and Italian. His home was adorned with paintings and rarities collected during his two-year sojourn in Italy. When he chose, his manners were those of a finished gentleman of the old school, and his conversation singularly interesting. Yet, he preferred his native Doric, asserting a Yorkshire burr was as superior to a Cockney lisp as a bull's bellow to a rat's squeak.

Known by everyone for miles, his intimate acquaintances were few. Original himself, he had no taste for the ordinary. He spent hours with a shrewd workman or a sagacious old cottager, grudging a moment to a commonplace fine gentleman or a frivolous lady. He made exceptions, however, for a certain order of mind: plain, ingenuous, devoid of intellectuality, yet incapable of feeling disgust at his rudeness or analyzing his sarcasm. With such people, he was at ease, and they, submitting implicitly without reflecting on his superiority, were perfectly tractable. Their unthinking insensibility was as convenient to him as the chair he sat upon.

He was not entirely uncordial with Mr. Moore, holding a faint partiality for three reasons. First, Moore's foreign accent and anti-Yorkshire appearance recalled Mr. Yorke's youthful travels in Italy and Paris, bringing back memories of his younger self. Second, he had known Moore's father, with whom his firm had been connected and implicated in losses. Third, he found Robert a sharp businessman, respecting his resolution and acuteness. Finally, as a guardian of the minor on whose estate Hollow's Mill stood, they consulted frequently on improvements.

With Mr. Helstone, however, existed a double antipathy: that of nature and circumstance. The free-thinker hated the formalist; the lover of liberty detested the disciplinarian. Rumor also whispered that in former years, they had been rival suitors of the same lady.

Mr. Yorke, once noted for preferring sprightly, dashing women with lively wits, had suddenly fallen in love with a girl who presented a complete contrast: Mary Cave, a girl of living marble, stillness personified. She answered in monosyllables, returned no glances, and rarely smiled. Yet, for him, she was perfect. He loved her for some reason he could not articulate.

Mr. Helstone, then curate of Briarfield, also fancied Mary. She was beautiful as a monumental angel, but the clergyman was preferred for his office, which invested him with an illusion Miss Cave did not find in the wool-staplers. Unlike Yorke's absorbing passion, Helstone saw her more as she really was. He was master of her and himself. She accepted him at the first offer, and they were married.

Nature never intended Mr. Helstone to be a good husband to a quiet wife. He believed silence meant contentment. If she did not complain of solitude, it could not be irksome. He made no pretense of understanding women, viewing them as an inferior order. After a year or two, his wife became of little importance to him. When she died, leaving only a still, beautiful-featured mold of clay in the conjugal couch, he felt his bereavement, though he shed no tears. His dry-eyed, sober mourning scandalized an old housekeeper and a female attendant who knew her capacity for feeling better than her husband. They gossiped over the corpse, working themselves up to indignation against the austere little man examining papers in the next room.

Hardly under the sod, rumors rife that she died of a broken heart magnified into reports of hard usage and harsh treatment. Grossly untrue, these stories were eagerly received. Mr. Yorke heard them and partly believed them. Having no friendly feeling for his successful rival, and remembering his own great disappointment, he conceived a rooted, bitter animosity toward Helstone for neglecting what would have been precious to him.

Mr. Helstone was half-aware of this animosity, unaware of how much Yorke had loved Mary or the calumnies concerning his treatment of her. He believed only political and religious differences separated them. Had he known the truth, he would not have crossed Yorke's threshold.

Mr. Yorke did not resume his lecture to Robert Moore. Soon, the conversation turned general, yet disputative, fueled by the unquiet state of the country and recent depredations on mill-property. The three gentlemen differed in their views. Mr. Helstone thought the masters aggrieved and the workpeople unreasonable, condemning the spirit of disaffection. He prescribed vigorous government interference, strict magisterial vigilance, and prompt military coercion.

Mr. Yorke asked if such measures would feed the hungry or give work to the unemployed. He scouted the idea of inevitable evils, declaring public patience a camel whose back was already laden to the limit; resistance was now a duty. He viewed the spirit of disaffection as the most promising sign of the times. The masters were aggrieved, but their grievances were heaped upon them by a "corrupt, base, and bloody" government. Madmen like Pitt, demons like Castlereagh, and mischievous idiots like Perceval were the tyrants whose ruinous war and oppressive taxation hung a millstone about England's neck.

"But where was the use of talking?" Yorke demanded. "What chance was there of reason being heard in a land king-ridden, priest-ridden, peer-ridden? Where a lunatic was nominal monarch, an unprincipled debauchee the real ruler? Where hereditary legislators and a bench of bishops were endured and venerated? Where a standing army was maintained to keep lazy parsons and their pauper families on the fat of the land?"

Mr. Helstone, rising to put on his shovel-hat, replied that he had seen sentiments like Yorke's bravely maintained only when allied with health and prosperity. But there came a time when the keepers of the house trembled. That was the test of the advocate of anarchy. He had read prayers for the dying bed of one of her most rancorous foes, stricken with remorse and unable to find a place for repentance. He warned Yorke that blasphemy against God and the king was a deadly sin and that there was judgment to come.

Yorke agreed fully that judgment to come existed. Otherwise, how could the scoundrels who broke innocent hearts with impunity, abused privileges, and took bread from the poor be properly paid off? But whenever he grew low-spirited about the seeming success of such evil in this mucky lump of a planet, he reached down to the old book, opened it at random, and found a verse blazing with blue brimstone that set all straight. He knew where some folk were bound, just as well as if an angel with great white wings had come over the door-stone to tell him.

"Sir," said Mr. Helstone, collecting his dignity, "the great knowledge of man is to know himself, and the bourne whither his own steps tend."

"Ay, ay. You'll recollect, Mr. Helstone, that Ignorance was carried away from the very gates of heaven, borne through the air, and thrust in at a door in the side of the hill which led down to hell."

"Nor have I forgotten, Mr. Yorke, that Vain-Confidence, not seeing the way before him, fell into a deep pit, which was on purpose there made by the prince of the grounds, to catch vainglorious fools withal, and was dashed to pieces with his fall."

"Now," interposed Mr. Moore, who had sat a silent but amused spectator, indifferent to the party politics and neighborhood gossip, making him an impartial, if apathetic, judge, "you have both sufficiently blackballed each other, and proved how cordially you detest each other, and how wicked you think each other. For my part, my hate is still running in such a strong current against the fellows who have broken my frames that I have none to spare for my private acquaintance, and still less for such a vague thing as a sect or a government. But really, gentlemen, you both seem very bad by your own showing—worse than ever I suspected you to be. I dare not stay all night with a rebel and blasphemer like you, Yorke; and I hardly dare ride home with a cruel and tyrannical ecclesiastic like Mr. Helstone."

"I am going, however, Mr. Moore," said the rector sternly. "Come with me or not, as you please."

"Nay, he shall not have the choice; he *shall* go with you," responded Yorke. "It's midnight, and past; and I'll have nob'dy staying up i' my house any longer. Ye mun all go."

He rang the bell.

"Deb," he said to the servant who answered, "clear them folk out o' t' kitchen, and lock t' doors, and be off to bed." Turning to his guests, he continued, "Here is your way, gentlemen," and, lighting them through the passage, he fairly put them out at his front door.

They met their party hurrying out pell-mell by the back way. Their horses stood at the gate; they mounted, and rode off, Moore laughing at their abrupt dismissal, Helstone deeply indignant thereat.

The morning light filtered weakly through the frost-rimed windows of Hollow's Cottage, casting long, grey shadows across the floorboards. Inside, the air was thick with the scent of brewing coffee and the faint, sweet perfume of stewed pears. Robert Moore sat at the small table, his posture rigid, his expression a mask of stoic indifference. Beside him, Hortense bustled with a frantic energy that seemed to vibrate against the stillness of the room. She was a woman of striking presence—tall, stout, and draped in the distinctive, somewhat unconventional attire of her homeland: a short petticoat and a striped cotton camisole, her black hair twisted up in curl-papers. Her face, animated by a perpetual mixture of irritation and self-importance, turned sharply toward her brother.

"Quelle idée! To destroy them," Hortense exclaimed, her voice rising in a crescendo of indignant French. "Quelle action honteuse! On voyait bien que les ouvriers de ce pays étaient à la fois bêtes et méchants. It was absolutely like the English servants, especially the maids! Think of that Sara! Nothing is more unbearable!"

Robert took a slow sip of his coffee, his gaze fixed on the steam rising from his cup. "She looks clean and industrious," he remarked calmly, his tone devoid of the passion his sister expected.

"Looks! I don't know how she looks, and I do not say that she is altogether dirty or idle, *mais* elle est d'une insolence!" Hortense waved a hand dismissively. "She disputed with me a quarter of an hour yesterday about the cooking of the beef. She said I boiled it to rags, that English people would never be able to eat such a dish as our *bouilli*, that the *bouillon* was no better than greasy warm water, and as to the *choucroute*, she affirms she cannot touch it! That barrel we have in the cellar—delightfully prepared by my own hands—she termed a tub of hog-wash, which means food for pigs. I am harassed with the girl, and yet I cannot part with her lest I should get a worse. You are in the same position with your workmen, *pauvre cher frère*!"

"I am afraid you are not very happy in England, Hortense."

"It is my duty to be happy where you are, brother; but otherwise there are certainly a thousand things which make me regret our native town. All the world here appears to me ill-bred (*mal-élevé*). I find my habits considered ridiculous. If a girl out of your mill chances to come into the kitchen and find me in my *jupon* and camisole preparing dinner, she sneers. If I accept an invitation out to tea, which I have done once or twice, I perceive I am put quite into the background; I have not that attention paid me which decidedly is my due. Of what an excellent family are the Gérards, as we know, and the Moores also! They have a right to claim a certain respect, and to feel wounded when it is withheld from them. In Antwerp I was always treated with distinction; here, one would think that when I open my lips in company I speak English with a ridiculous accent, whereas I am quite assured that I pronounce it perfectly."

"Hortense, in Antwerp we were known rich; in England we were never known but poor."

"Precisely, and thus mercenary are mankind. Again, dear brother, last Sunday, if you recollect, was very wet; accordingly I went to church in my neat black sabots, objects one would indeed not wear in a fashionable city, but which in the country I have ever been accustomed to use for walking in dirty roads. Believe me, as I paced up the aisle, composed and tranquil, as I am always, four ladies, and as many gentlemen, laughed and hid their faces behind their prayer-books."

"Well, well! don't put on the sabots again. I told you before I thought they were not quite the thing for this country."

"But, brother, they are not common sabots, such as the peasantry wear. I tell you, they are *sabots noirs*, très propres, très convenables. At Mons and Leuze—cities not very far removed from the elegant capital of Brussels—it is very seldom that the respectable people wear anything else for walking in winter. Let any one try to wade the mud of the Flemish *chaussées* in a pair of Paris brodequins, on *m'en dirait des nouvelles*!"

"Never mind Mons and Leuze and the Flemish *chaussées*; do at Rome as the Romans do. And as to the camisole and *jupon*, I am not quite sure about them either. I never see an English lady dressed in such garments. Ask Caroline Helstone."

"Caroline! *I* ask Caroline? *I* consult her about my dress? It is *she* who on all points should consult *me*. She is a child."

"She is eighteen, or at least seventeen—old enough to know all about gowns, petticoats, and *chaussures*."

"Do not spoil Caroline, I entreat you, brother. Do not make her of more consequence than she ought to be. At present she is modest and unassuming: let us keep her so."

"With all my heart. Is she coming this morning?"

"She will come at ten, as usual, to take her French lesson."

"You don't find that she sneers at you, do you?"

"She does not. She appreciates me better than any one else here; but then she has more intimate opportunities of knowing me. She sees that I have education, intelligence, manner, principles—all, in short, which belongs to a person well born and well bred."

"Are you at all fond of her?"

"For *fond* I cannot say. I am not one who is prone to take violent fancies, and, consequently, my friendship is the more to be depended on. I have a regard for her as my relative; her position also inspires interest, and her conduct as my pupil has hitherto been such as rather to enhance than diminish the attachment that springs from other causes."

"She behaves pretty well at lessons?"

"To *me* she behaves very well; but you are conscious, brother, that I have a manner calculated to repel over-familiarity, to win esteem, and to command respect. Yet, possessed of penetration, I perceive clearly that Caroline is not perfect, that there is much to be desired in her."

"Give me a last cup of coffee, and while I am drinking it amuse me with an account of her faults."

"Dear brother, I am happy to see you eat your breakfast with relish, after the fatiguing night you have passed. Caroline, then, is defective; but with my forming hand and almost motherly care she may improve. There is about her an occasional something—a reserve, I think—which I do not quite like, because it is not sufficiently girlish and submissive; and there are glimpses of an unsettled hurry in her nature, which put me out. Yet she is usually most tranquil, too dejected and thoughtful indeed sometimes. In time, I doubt not, I shall make her uniformly sedate and decorous, without being unaccountably pensive. I ever disapprove what is not intelligible."

"I don't understand your account in the least. What do you mean by 'unsettled hurries,' for instance?"

"An example will, perhaps, be the most satisfactory explanation. I sometimes, you are aware, make her read French poetry by way of practice in pronunciation. She has in the course of her lessons gone through much of Corneille and Racine, in a very steady, sober spirit, such as I approve. Occasionally she showed, indeed, a degree of languor in the perusal of those esteemed authors, partaking rather of apathy than sobriety; and apathy is what I cannot tolerate in those who have the benefit of my instructions—besides, one should not be apathetic in studying standard works. The other day I put into her hands a volume of short fugitive pieces. I sent her to the window to learn one by heart, and when I looked up I saw her turning the leaves over impatiently, and curling her lip, absolutely with scorn, as she surveyed the little poems cursorily. I chid her. 'Ma cousine,' said she, 'tout cela m'ennuie à la mort.' I told her this was improper language. 'Dieu!' she exclaimed, 'il n'y a donc pas deux lignes de poésie dans toute la littérature française?' I inquired what she meant. She begged my pardon with proper submission. Ere long she was still. I saw her smiling to herself over the book. She began to learn assiduously. In half an hour she came and stood before me, presented the volume, folded her hands, as I always require her to do, and commenced the repetition of that short thing by Chénier, 'La Jeune Captive.' If you had heard the manner in which she went through this, and in which she uttered a few incoherent comments when she had done, you would have known what I meant by the phrase 'unsettled hurry.' One would have thought Chénier was more moving than all Racine and all Corneille. You, brother, who have so much sagacity, will discern that this disproportionate preference argues an ill-regulated mind; but she is fortunate in her preceptress. I will give her a system, a method of thought, a set of opinions; I will give her the perfect control and guidance of her feelings."

"Be sure you do, Hortense. Here she comes. That was her shadow passed the window, I believe."

"Ah! truly. She is too early—half an hour before her time.—My child, what brings you here before I have breakfasted?"

This question was addressed to an individual who now entered the room, a young girl wrapped in a winter mantle, the folds of which were gathered with some grace round an apparently slender figure.

"I came in haste to see how you were, Hortense, and how Robert was too. I was sure you would be both grieved by what happened last night. I did not hear till this morning. My uncle told me at breakfast."

"Ah! it is unspeakable. You sympathize with us? Your uncle sympathizes with us?"

"My uncle is very angry—but he was with Robert, I believe, was he not?—Did he not go with you to Stilbro' Moor?"

"Yes, we set out in very martial style, Caroline; but the prisoners we went to rescue met us half-way."

"Of course nobody was hurt?"

"Why, no; only Joe Scott's wrists were a little galled with being pinioned too tightly behind his back."

"You were not there? You were not with the wagons when they were attacked?"

"No. One seldom has the fortune to be present at occurrences at which one would particularly wish to assist."

"Where are you going this morning? I saw Murgatroyd saddling your horse in the yard."

"To Whinbury. It is market day."

"Mr. Yorke is going too. I met him in his gig. Come home with him."

"Why?"

"Two are better than one, and nobody dislikes Mr. Yorke—at least, poor people do not dislike him."

"Therefore he would be a protection to me, who am hated?"

"Who are *misunderstood*. That, probably, is the word. Shall you be late?—Will he be late, Cousin Hortense?"

"It is too probable. He has often much business to transact at Whinbury. Have you brought your exercise-book, child?"

"Yes.—What time will you return, Robert?"

"I generally return at seven. Do you wish me to be at home earlier?"

"Try rather to be back by six. It is not absolutely dark at six now, but by seven daylight is quite gone."

"And what danger is to be apprehended, Caroline, when daylight *is* gone? What peril do you conceive comes as the companion of darkness for me?"

"I am not sure that I can define my fears, but we all have a certain anxiety at present about our friends. My uncle calls these times dangerous. He says, too, that mill-owners are unpopular."

"And I am one of the most unpopular? Is not that the fact? You are reluctant to speak out plainly, but at heart you think me liable to Pearson's fate, who was shot at—not, indeed, from behind a hedge, but in his own house, through his staircase window, as he was going to bed."

"Anne Pearson showed me the bullet in the chamber-door," remarked Caroline gravely, as she folded her mantle and arranged it and her muff on a side-table. "You know," she continued, "there is a hedge all the way along the road from here to Whinbury, and there are the Fieldhead plantations to pass; but you will be back by six—or before?"

"Certainly he will," affirmed Hortense. "And now, my child, prepare your lessons for repetition, while I put the peas to soak for the purée at dinner."

With this direction she left the room.

"You suspect I have many enemies, then, Caroline," said Mr. Moore, "and doubtless you know me to be destitute of friends?"

"Not destitute, Robert. There is your sister, your brother Louis, whom I have never seen; there is Mr. Yorke, and there is my uncle—besides, of course, many more."

Robert smiled. "You would be puzzled to name your 'many more,'" said he. "But show me your exercise-book. What extreme pains you take with the writing! My sister, I suppose, exacts this care. She wants to form you in all things after the model of a Flemish school-girl. What life are you destined for, Caroline? What will you do with your French, drawing, and other accomplishments, when they are acquired?"

"You may well say, when they are acquired; for, as you are aware, till Hortense began to teach me, I knew precious little. As to the life I am destined for, I cannot tell.

The morning sun filtered through the windowpane, warming the small room where Caroline Helstone sat with her eyes fixed on a point somewhere beyond the glass. Her governess, Mademoiselle Moore, watched her with a critical eye, noting the pupil's recurring lapse into silence. Caroline forgot the explanations offered to her, her mind drifting despite the chiding that followed. Yet, she bore the reproofs with a serene, unclouded patience. Bathed in the sunlight, she seemed to absorb a gentle influence that rendered her both happy and kind. In this state, she was at her most pleasing.

Caroline possessed a beauty that required no introduction to be appreciated. She was fair enough to captivate at first sight, her figure light and pliant, every curve neat and proportionate. Her face was expressive and gentle, framed by a profusion of brown curls that she arranged with taste. Her eyes held a winning beam that spoke softly to the affections, and her mouth was very pretty. She wore a simple winter garb of merino in a soft brown shade that matched her hair, accented only by a pink ribbon and knot at her neck. Her style was unobtrusive yet tasteful, suiting her slight frame perfectly.

While her appearance was undeniable, her intellect remained a mystery for the present. Her origins were soon revealed: the daughter of parents separated shortly after her birth due to incompatible dispositions. Her mother, the half-sister of Mr. Moore's father, had been taken from her in infancy. Her father, the brother of the rector, was a man whose character friends preferred not to recall after death; he had rendered his wife unhappy, and rumors of his conduct had tainted the reputation of his more principled brother. Having known neither parent, Caroline had been raised solely by her uncle, the rector. He was ill-suited to guide a young girl, neglecting her education until she herself grew anxious to acquire knowledge. Consequently, she felt inferior to her peers, a sentiment that made her eagerly accept the offer from her cousin Hortense to teach her French and needlework.

Mademoiselle Moore, proud of her "positive spirit" and preference for dry studies, delighted in the task. She viewed Caroline as an irregularly taught, nearly ignorant girl, attributing the pupil's rapid progress entirely to her own superior methods. Hortense kept her young cousin closely bound to the grammar of the French language, assigning endless "logical analyses." These exercises were a source of little pleasure to Caroline, who grudgingly spent hours pondering propositions and incident clauses. Occasionally, lost in the maze of syntax, she would seek aid from Robert in the counting-house. Mr. Moore, with his clear and tranquil mind, could dissolve her difficulties in moments, offering keys to puzzles that baffled Hortense. Caroline admired him deeply, wishing she were a boy so she might sit with him as a clerk rather than endure Hortense's lessons in the parlour.

Evenings at Hollow's Cottage were rare but precious. When Robert was home and free from business, the hours passed swiftly. Hortense, usually formal and self-important, relaxed in the presence of her kin, becoming kind and musical when she played the guitar. Robert, though not naturally lively, was a willing spectator of Caroline's vivacity, a complacent listener to her talk. Yet, these moments were fleeting; by the next morning, Robert was often "frozen up" again, rarely contriving their recurrence. This puzzled Hortense, who believed happiness should be cultivated constantly, yet she never dared to invite Caroline unasked, waiting for Robert's cue.

This morning marked a change. For the first time, Robert had invited Caroline unprompted, speaking with such kindness that she carried a sense of happiness throughout the day. The morning passed as usual, filled with Hortense's bustling supervision and Caroline's diligent repetition. During dinner, Sarah, the mill worker, served a meal with disdain, calling it unfit for dogs, yet the food—pea purée, chopped meat, greens, and a fruit pâté—was savory enough. Caroline accepted the Belgian cookery without complaint, knowing that showing distaste would incur her cousin's wrath.

Afterward, Caroline managed to coax Hortense upstairs to dress. Avoiding any hint that the camisole and curl-papers were odious, she persuaded her cousin to make her toilet in the bedroom. While Hortense delivered a homily on her own disregard for fashion, Caroline undressed her, investing her in a decent gown and arranging her hair. However, Hortense insisted on finishing with a thick handkerchief and a voluminous black apron, symbols of morality and thrift to her. Caroline protested, citing suffocation and the adequacy of her own short aprons, but Robert intervened, overhearing the dispute. He deemed Caroline's attire sufficient for a child, compelling Hortense to yield, though she disapproved of the resulting neatness.

The afternoon was devoted to sewing. Hortense, skilled with her needle, condemned Caroline to learn the tedious art of darning stockings stitch by stitch, a duty she considered essential for women. Caroline found the task grievous, often sighing over the work, but she adhered to it, knowing that burning the stockings would only result in a fresh pair. As the sky darkened and rain poured down, suspense gripped Caroline. She feared Robert would be detained at the mill by Mr. Sykes or Mr. Yorke.

At five o'clock, with the clouds streaming and the wind whispering in the roof-trees, Mademoiselle Moore declared that Robert would not return until moonrise. "We will have coffee," she decided, dismissing the hope of his arrival. Caroline obeyed, retreating to the kitchen where Sarah was making a dress. Sarah noted Caroline's listlessness, suggesting she was fit to cry from being cooped up. When Caroline asked if her master often returned early in wet weather, Sarah revealed she had seen Murgatroyd lead Robert's horse into the yard and heard him speaking of new frames and guards.

Caroline dismissed Sarah's claims as mistaken, but Sarah persisted, claiming to have heard Robert's voice. "He is coming," Sarah announced as the kitchen door opened. Mr. Moore entered, wet and cold. Caroline, pretending to focus on her dressmaking, hid her face before turning to meet him, her countenance instantly beaming.

"We had ceased to expect you," she said. "They asserted you would not come."

"But I promised to return soon. You expected me, I suppose?"

"No, Robert; I dared not when it rained so fast. And you are wet and chilled. Change everything. If you took cold, we should blame ourselves."

"I am not wet through: my riding-coat is waterproof. Dry shoes are all I require."

Standing by the hearth, Robert enjoyed the fire's glow. His glance rested on Caroline's uplifted face, flushed and smiling, shaded by silky curls. Sarah withdrew to the parlour with the coffee tray. Robert placed a hand on Caroline's shoulder, stooping to kiss her forehead.

"Oh!" she exclaimed, as if the kiss had unsealed her lips. "I was miserable when I thought you would not come. I am almost too happy now. Are you happy, Robert? Do you like to come home?"

"I think I do—to-night, at least."

"Are you certain you are not fretting about your frames, your business, and the war?"

"Not just now."

"Are you positive you don't feel Hollow's Cottage too small for you, and narrow, and dismal?"

"At this moment, no."

"Can you affirm that you are not bitter at heart because rich and great people forget you?"

"No more questions. You are mistaken if you think I am anxious to curry favour with rich and great people. I only want means—a position—a career."

"Which your own talent and goodness shall win you. You were made to be great; you *shall* be great."

"I wonder now, if you spoke honestly out of your heart, what recipe you would give me for acquiring this same greatness; but I know it—better than you know it yourself. Would it be efficacious? Would it work? Yes—poverty, misery, bankruptcy. Oh, life is not what you think it, Lina!"

"But you are what I think you."

"I am not."

"You are better, then?"

"Far worse."

"No; far better. I know you are good."

"How do you know it?"

"You look so, and I feel you *are* so."

"Where do you feel it?"

"In my heart."

"Ah! You judge me with your heart, Lina: you should judge me with your head."

"I do; and then I am quite proud of you. Robert, you cannot tell all my thoughts about you."

Robert's dark face flushed; his lips smiled yet remained compressed, his eyes laughed while his brow knitted. "Think meanly of me, Lina," he said. "Men, in general, are a sort of scum, very different to anything of which you have an idea. I make no pretension to be better than my fellows."

"If you did, I should not esteem you so much. It is because you are modest that I have such confidence in your merit."

"Are you flattering me?" he demanded, turning sharply upon her with an eye of acute penetration.

"No," she said softly, laughing at his sudden quickness. She seemed to think it unnecessary to proffer any eager disavowal.

"You don't care whether I think you flatter me or not?"

"No."

"You are so secure of your own intentions?"

"I suppose so."

"What are they, Caroline?"

"Only to ease my mind by expressing for once part of what I think, and then to make you better satisfied with yourself."

"By assuring me that my kinswoman is my sincere friend?"

"Just so. I am your sincere friend, Robert."

"And I am—what chance and change shall make me, Lina."

"Not my enemy, however?"

The answer was cut short by Sarah and her mistress entering the kitchen in commotion. They had been arguing over "café au lait," which Sarah deemed a waste of God's gifts, while Mademoiselle affirmed it was a royal beverage. The former occupants withdrew to the parlour. Before Hortense followed, Caroline questioned Robert once more, "Not my enemy, Robert?" Moore, Quaker-like, replied with another query, "Could I be?" and then seated himself beside her.

Caroline scarcely heard Hortense's explosion of wrath upon their reunion; the long declamation about the "indign conduct of this wicked creature" sounded confusedly like rattling china. Robert laughed in subdued tones, politely entreating his sister to be tranquil. He assured her that if she desired satisfaction, she could choose any girl from the mill as an attendant, though he feared they were mostly ignorant of household work. Hortense admitted the truth of this, complaining of the "insupportable" English peasants and wishing for an Antwerp girl. Robert, stern in public but kind in private, offered to fetch her an Antwerp servant, but Hortense declined, noting such a girl would not stay long amidst the factory's young coquettes. She softened, acknowledging her domestic trials as her destiny, recalling her mother's similar sufferings in Antwerp. Robert, remembering his mother's hot kitchen in Belgium, let the subject drop.

When the coffee-service was removed, Robert fetched Hortense's music-book and guitar. Arranging the ribbon around her neck with fraternal kindness, he asked for her mother's favorite songs. Nothing refines like affection; family union elevates. Hortense, pleased and grateful, looked almost graceful, her fretful look replaced by a "sourire plein de bonté." She sang with feeling, the songs reminding her of her young days and a parent she had loved. Observing Caroline's naïve interest, Hortense's good-humour increased, and the exclamation, "I wish I could sing and play like Hortense!" rendered her charming for the evening.

A lecture followed on the vanity of wishing versus the duty of trying. "As Rome," it was suggested, "had not been built in a day, so neither had Mademoiselle Gérard Moore's education been completed in a week, or by merely *wishing* to be clever. It was effort that had accomplished that great work." Once on the theme of her own merits, Hortense was fluent, taking her knitting and sitting down tranquil in the blissful self-complacency of the warm parlour.

"What shall we do now, Caroline?" asked Mr. Moore, returning to his seat.

"What shall we do, Robert?" she repeated playfully. "You decide."

"Not play at chess?"

"No."

"Nor draughts, nor backgammon?"

"No, no; we both hate silent games that only keep one's hands employed, don't we?"

"I believe we do. Then shall we talk scandal?"

"About whom? Are we sufficiently interested in anybody to take a pleasure in pulling their character to pieces?"

"A question that comes to the point. For my part, unamiable as it sounds, I must say no."

"And I too. But it is strange, though we want no third—fourth, I mean (she hastily and with contrition glanced at Hortense)—living person among us—so selfish we are in our happiness—though we don't want to think of the present existing world, it would be pleasant to go back to the past, to hear people that have slept for generations in graves that are perhaps no longer graves now, but gardens and fields, speak to us and tell us their thoughts, and impart their ideas."

"Who shall be the speaker? What language shall he utter? French?"

"Your French forefathers don't speak so sweetly, nor so solemnly, nor so impressively as your English ancestors, Robert. To-night you shall be entirely English. You shall read an English book."

"An old English book?"

"Yes, an old English book—one that you like; and I will choose a part of it that is toned quite in harmony with something in you. It shall waken your nature, fill your mind with music; it shall pass like a skilful hand over your heart, and make its strings sound. Your heart is a lyre, Robert; but the lot of your life has not been a minstrel to sweep it, and it is often silent. Let glorious William come near

Eighteen is the threshold where the fairy tale ends and the real world begins. Until then, life is a story we listen to—a marvelous fiction filled with heroes, demons, and enchanted landscapes. The moon is larger, the sun burns with divine fire, and the dangers are merely shadows in a dream. But at eighteen, the shores of Reality rise before us, blue and soft yet terrifyingly distant. We long to reach them, imagining a land where hunger and sorrow do not exist. Yet, every joy must be earned through a bloody struggle; the heart must be gilded with the sweat of combat before the wreath of victory can be worn.

At eighteen, we are blind to this. Hope smiles, promising a tomorrow of endless happiness. Love wanders in like a lost angel, welcomed with open arms. We do not see the quiver in his bow, nor the barb hidden in his arrow; we mistake the wound for a thrill of new life. We believe passion is an unqualified good, unaware that we are about to enter the school of Experience. Alas, Experience! No other teacher wears a face so frozen, a robe so black, or wields a rod so heavy. She is the only guide through life's wilds, forcing the novice to stumble and stray until she finds a safe path.

Caroline Helstone, just turned eighteen, had no desire to spend the evening with her uncle. The rectory study was sacred ground to her, a place she rarely intruded upon. She stayed away until the bell rang for prayers. The evening service was a ritual in the Helstone household, conducted by Mr. Helstone in his usual nasal, monotonous voice.
