Once the rite was over, his niece approached him.

"Good-night, uncle."

"Hey! You've been gadding abroad all day—visiting, dining out, and what not!"

"Only at the cottage."

"And have you learned your lessons?"

"Yes."

"And made a shirt?"

"Only part of one."

"Well, that will do. Stick to the needle, learn shirt-making and gown-making and piecrust-making, and you'll be a clever woman some day. Go to bed now. I'm busy with a pamphlet here."

Soon, Caroline was locked in her small bedroom. She donned her white dressing-gown, loosened her long, wavy hair, and leaned against her hand, staring at the carpet. Around her rose the visions of youth. Her thoughts spoke pleasantly; she smiled as she listened. To her, the spirit of Hope was a brighter presence in the room. This flattering prophet promised no more disappointment, only the true spring of morning. She could not suspect she was the sport of delusion. Her expectations seemed solid, warranted by the solid ground of her own heart.

"When people love, the next step is they marry," she reasoned. "Now, I love Robert, and I feel sure that Robert loves me. When I looked up at him after repeating Chénier's poem, his eyes sent the truth through my heart. Sometimes I am afraid to speak to him, lest I seem forward, lest I say too much and he disapprove. But tonight, he was so indulgent. How kind he was walking up the lane! His love-making—friendship, I mean—is not like the books; it is original, quiet, manly, sincere. I *do* like him. I would be an excellent wife to him. I would tell him of his faults, study his comfort, and cherish him. Now, I am sure he will not be cold tomorrow. I feel almost certain he will come here, or ask me to go there."

She combed her hair, long as a mermaid's, and glanced at her reflection in the glass. While plain people see no enchantment in their own image, the fair believe they must charm. Caroline saw a shape and head that, captured in that moment, would be lovely. The mirror confirmed her hopes, and she sought her couch in undiminished gladness.

She rose the next day with the same gladness. Entering her uncle's breakfast room, she greeted him with soft cheerfulness. Even the little man of bronze thought, for an instant, that his niece was becoming "a fine girl." Usually quiet and timid with him, she found many things to say today. She spoke of flowers blooming in the garden, the gardener's schedule, and the starlings nesting in the church tower.

Mr. Helstone opined that the birds were "like other fools who had just paired—insensible to inconvenience just for the moment." Emboldened by her good spirits, Caroline ventured a remark she had never dared before.

"Uncle," she said, "whenever you speak of marriage you speak of it scornfully. Do you think people shouldn't marry?"

"It is decidedly the wisest plan to remain single, especially for women."

"Are all marriages unhappy?"

"Millions of marriages are unhappy. If everybody confessed the truth, perhaps all are more or less so."

"You are always vexed when you are asked to come and marry a couple. Why?"

"Because one does not like to act as accessory to the commission of a piece of pure folly."

Mr. Helstone seemed glad to vent his mind. Caroline pressed further.

"But why," she asked, "should it be pure folly? If two people like each other, why shouldn't they consent to live together?"

"They tire of each other—they tire of each other in a month. A yokefellow is not a companion; he or she is a fellow-sufferer."

Caroline's reply was not naive; it was born of antipathy to his cynicism.

"One would think you had never been married, uncle. One would think you were an old bachelor."

"Practically, I am so."

"But you have been married. Why were you so inconsistent as to marry?"

"Every man is mad once or twice in his life."

"So you tired of my aunt, and my aunt of you, and you were miserable together?"

Mr. Helstone pushed out his cynical lip, wrinkled his brown forehead, and grunted.

"Did she not suit you? Was she not good-tempered? Did you not get used to her? Were you not sorry when she died?"

"Caroline," Mr. Helstone said, slamming his hand on the mahogany table, "understand this: it is vulgar and puerile to confound generals with particulars. In every case there is the rule and there are the exceptions. Your questions are stupid and babyish. Ring the bell, if you have done breakfast."

The breakfast was cleared away. Instead of leaving, Caroline sat by the window. Mr. Helstone looked at her uneasily but continued reading his paper, which featured dispatches from General Lord Wellington. He did not know that tumultuous thoughts were swirling in his niece's mind, thoughts revived by their conversation but planted years ago.

She reviewed his character, his sentiments on marriage, and the wide chasm between her mind and his. Across that chasm stood another figure, dim and sinister—the memory of her father, James Helstone. Rumors had reached her of his bad character. She remembered dark weeks spent with him in a great town, shut up in a high garret without a carpet, while he went out early and forgot to return. He had been furious, terrible, or imbecile. She recalled one night when he had raved, saying he would kill her because she was a burden. Her screams had brought aid, and she had never seen him again except as a dead man in his coffin.

She also had a mother, though Mr. Helstone never spoke of her. Caroline knew she was alive, but she was the drunkard's wife. Turning from the lattice, she broke the silence in a low, bitter voice.

"You term marriage miserable, I suppose, from what you saw of my father and mother's. If my mother suffered what I suffered when I was with papa, she must have had a dreadful life."

Mr. Helstone wheeled about, startled. He could not conceive that she had any thoughts or memories of her parents.

"Your father and mother? Who has been talking to you about them?"

"Nobody; but I remember something of what papa was, and I pity mamma. Where is she?"

"I hardly know," Mr. Helstone replied, avoiding the question. "I was little acquainted with her. I have not heard from her for years... Come, it is school-time. You go to your cousin at ten, don't you? The clock has struck."

Fanny entered to announce that the churchwardens needed Mr. Helstone. He hastened away, and Caroline set out for the cottage.

The road to Hollow's Mill inclined downward, and she ran almost the entire way. The fresh air and the thought of Robert revived her spirits. Upon arriving near the white house, she saw Moore at his garden gate. He stood in his belted Holland blouse and light cap.

"He has not his peer," she thought. "He is as handsome as he is intelligent. What a keen eye he has! What clearly-cut, spirited features... better than any of those shuffling curates... better than anybody; bonny Robert!"

She sought him out. When she challenged his sight, he would have vanished like a phantom if he could, but he was a tall fact and had to stand the greeting. His manner was brief—cousin-like, brother-like, friend-like, but anything but lover-like. The nameless charm of the previous night had vanished. He was no longer the same man.

Rude disappointment struck her. A lover masculine might demand explanation, but a lover feminine could say nothing. To speak would bring shame and inward remorse. Nature brands such demonstration as rebellion, repaying it later with the thunderbolt of self-contempt. The wise course is to endure. You expected bread and got a stone; break your teeth on it and do not shriek. You held out for an egg and received a scorpion; close your fingers firmly upon the gift and let it sting. In time, the scorpion dies, and you learn to endure without a sob.

Half-bitterness is strength; it is a tonic. Sweet force following acute suffering is a delusion. Energy remaining after the rack is dangerous, deadly when confronted with injustice.

Caroline recalled the ballad of "Puir Mary Lee," the peasant girl driven from her home by a false lover. Mary sits in the snowstorm, cursing Robin-a-Ree. But what of Caroline? Robert had done her no wrong; he had told her no lie. She was to blame for loving without being asked. Robert had seemed fond, but he was withdrawing because he did not wish his affections entangled in a marriage he deemed imprudent.

What was she to do? Pursue him and lose his esteem, or turn upon herself? If she had sense, she would be her own governor, resolving to subdue the disturbed realm of her emotions. She would look on life steadily, learning its severe truths.

It appeared she had sense. She quitted Robert quietly, without complaint or tear, and betook herself to her studies under Hortense. At dinner, she went home without lingering.

Later, in the rectory drawing-room, alone with her uncle drinking port, she faced a new difficulty: "How am I to get through this day?" She had hoped for another evening with Robert, but her mistake was learned. She could not settle down, convinced no chance would recall him.

He had visited twice before, treating her with peculiar reserve, yet looking at her relentingly. False Hope whispered he might come tonight. She tried to read, but her thoughts wandered. She tried to sew, finding every stitch tedious. She attempted a French composition and wrote only mistakes.

Suddenly, the door-bell rang sharply. Her heart leaped. She peered through the aperture. Fanny was admitting a visitor—a tall gentleman, just the height of Robert. For one second she thought it was him, exulting; but the voice asking for Mr. Helstone undeceived her. It was an Irish voice—Curate Malone.

It was a fact that at whatever house a curate dropped in, another soon followed. They were all on the run, chasing each other from lodging to lodging. Thus, Caroline's ears were tortured three times by the bell and the advent of undesired guests: Malone, followed by Donne, followed by Sweeting. More wine was ordered, and through the closed doors, Caroline heard their boyish laughter and vacant cackle. Her fear was lest they stay for tea, for she had no pleasure in serving that particular trio.

Fortune brought four more guests: Lady Sykes and her three buxom daughters, arriving in a pony-phaeton from Whinbury. The bell clanged a fourth time. Fanny announced, "Mrs. Sykes and the three Misses Sykes."

When receiving company, Caroline's habit was to wring her hands nervously, flush, and hurry forward, wishing herself at Jericho. She was deficient in finished manner. Accordingly, her small white hands maltreated each other as she waited.

In stalked Mrs. Sykes, a tall, bilious gentlewoman professing ample piety. In sailed her three daughters, a showy trio. In English country ladies, there is a certain expression stamped on the features that says, "I know I am the standard of what is proper; let everyone therefore whom I approach keep a sharp lookout, for wherein they differ from me, therein they are wrong."

Mrs. and Misses Sykes were pointed illustrations of this truth. Miss Mary wore her complacency with state; Miss Harriet carried it overbearingly, looking high and cold; Miss Hannah, conceited and dashing, flourished hers openly. The mother evinced it with the gravity proper to her age.

The reception was got through somehow. Caroline lied, saying she was glad to see them, hoping Mrs. Sykes's cough was better (it had lasted twenty years), and hoping the Misses Sykes had left their sisters well. The Misses Sykes replied with a simultaneous, majestic bow that ensured silence for the next five minutes.

Mrs. Sykes inquired after Mr. Helstone's rheumatism and preaching fatigue. On being assured he was well, they chorused that he was a "wonderful man of his years."

Miss Mary then asked if Caroline had attended the Bible Society meeting at N

The morning light had barely touched the horizon when Moore rose, his movements swift and silent. Before his sister could prepare the coffee or slice the toast, he had already ridden out to Whinbury and returned, the purpose of his errand locked safely within his own mind. Hortense, accustomed to his enigmatic nature, offered no inquiry; she understood that the complexities of commerce were mysteries best kept buried, surfacing only occasionally to frighten the local clerk or startle a visiting journalist. A profound reserve regarding his affairs seemed woven into the very fabric of his mercantile soul.

Once breakfast was consumed, Moore descended to his counting-house. Henry, Joe Scott's boy, deposited the morning mail and newspapers upon the desk. Moore sat, breaking the seals of the letters with a brisk efficiency. He scanned them quickly, his expression unreadable until he reached the final document. Then, a derisive snort escaped his nostrils—a sound of defiance. Though he spoke no words, his eyes darkened with a look that seemed to summon the devil, charging him to consume the entire enterprise in hellfire. With a sudden spasm of fury limited strictly to his fingers, he snapped the pen's feathered top and began to draft a series of sharp replies. Sealed and dispatched, he stepped out to walk through the mill, returning shortly thereafter to stare blankly at his newspaper.

The news held little power over him. He folded the paper across his knees, arms crossed, gazing into the hearth. Occasionally, his gaze drifted to the window, then to his watch. His mind was clearly elsewhere, perhaps lost in the beauty of the crisp, mild air, wishing he were out in the fields rather than trapped indoors. The door to the counting-house stood wide open, inviting the breeze and sunlight, yet the only scent that drifted in was not of spring, but of sulfur, carried on the black plume of smoke rising from the gaunt chimney.

Suddenly, a dark-blue figure appeared at the threshold—Joe Scott, fresh from a dyeing vat. "He's comed, sir," he announced, vanishing as quickly as he arrived.

Mr. Moore did not look up. A massive man, clad in heavy fustian and gray worsted stockings, entered. He was broad-shouldered and imposing, wearing a hat of questionable quality which he promptly shoved under his chair. Wiping his forehead with a spotted handkerchief pulled from the crown of the hat, he remarked that it was "raight dahn warm for Febewerry." Moore offered a barely audible grunt of agreement. The visitor then placed an official-looking staff in the corner and whistled, feigning ease.

"You have what is necessary, I suppose?" Moore asked, finally looking up.

"Ay, ay! all's right."

The visitor resumed his whistling; Moore returned to his paper. Soon, however, Moore reached for a black bottle from his cupboard, a tumbler, and a jug of water. He placed them on the table.

"Help yourself; there's water in that jar in the corner," he instructed his guest.

"I dunnut knaw that there's mich need," the visitor replied, rising to pour a drink. "For all a body is dry in a morning."

"Will you tak naught yourseln, Mr. Moore?" he asked, mixing a portion and testing it with a deep draught before sinking back, satisfied.

Moore declined with a negative murmur.

"Yah'd as good," the visitor persisted. "It 'uld set ye up wald a sup o' this stuff. Uncommon good hollands. Ye get it fro' furrin parts, I'se think?"

"Ay!"

"Tak my advice and try a glass on't. Them lads 'at's coming 'll keep ye talking, nob'dy knows how long. Ye'll need propping."

"Have you seen Mr. Sykes this morning?" Moore interrupted.

"I seed him a hauf an hour sin', just afore I set off. He said he aimed to come here, and I sudn't wonder but ye'll have old Helstone too. I seed 'em saddling his little nag as I passed at back o' t' rectory."

The speaker proved a prophet. Five minutes later, the trot of a small horse's hooves echoed in the yard. A familiar nasal voice cried out, "Boy, take my horse and lead him into the stable."

Helstone entered, marching with nimble energy, looking browner and keener than usual. "Beautiful morning, Moore. How do, my boy? Ha! whom have we here?" he asked, turning to the man with the staff. "Sugden! What! you're going to work directly? On my word, you lose no time. But I come to ask explanations. Your message was delivered to me. Are you sure you are on the right scent? How do you mean to set about the business? Have you got a warrant?"

"Sugden has."

"Then you are going to seek him now? I'll accompany you."

"You will be spared that trouble, sir; he is coming to seek me. I'm just now sitting in state waiting his arrival."

"And who is it? One of my parishioners?"

Joe Scott had entered unobserved, now standing like a sinister phantom, half his body dyed a deep indigo, leaning heavily on the desk. His master's answer to the rector's question was a smile. Joe took the lead, adopting a quiet but mischievous expression.

"It's a friend of yours, Mr. Helstone, a gentleman you often speak of."

"Indeed! His name, Joe? You look well this morning."

"Only the Rev. Moses Barraclough; t' tub orator you call him sometimes, I think."

"Ah!" said the rector, producing his snuff-box and taking a long pinch. "Couldn't have supposed it. Why, the pious man never was a workman of yours, Moore. He's a tailor by trade."

"And so much the worse grudge I owe him, for interfering and setting my discarded men against me."

"And Moses was actually present at the battle of Stilbro' Moor? He went there, wooden leg and all?"

"Ay, sir," said Joe. "He went there on horseback, that his leg mightn't be noticed. He was the captain, and wore a mask. The rest only had their faces blackened."

"And how was he found out?"

"I'll tell you, sir," Joe continued. "T' maister's not so fond of talking. I've no objections. He courted Sarah, Mr. Moore's sarvant lass, and so it seems she would have nothing to say to him; she either didn't like his wooden leg or she'd some notion about his being a hypocrite. Happen, for women is queer hands... she'd have encouraged him, in spite of his leg and his deceit, just to pass time like. I've known some on 'em do as mich, and some o' t' bonniest and mimmest-looking, too..."

"Joe, you're insupportable," Mr. Moore cut in. "You spin out your explanation as Moses spins out his sermons. The long and short of it is, Murgatroyd was jealous of Barraclough; and last night, as he and a friend took shelter in a barn from a shower, they heard and saw Moses conferring with some associates within. From their discourse it was plain he had been the leader, not only at Stilbro' Moor, but in the attack on Sykes's property. Moreover they planned a deputation to wait on me this morning, which the tailor is to head, and which, in the most religious and peaceful spirit, is to entreat me to put the accursed thing out of my tent. I rode over to Whinbury this morning, got a constable and a warrant, and I am now waiting to give my friend the reception he deserves. Here, meantime, comes Sykes. Mr. Helstone, you must spirit him up. He feels timid at the thoughts of prosecuting."

A gig rolled into the yard. Mr. Sykes entered—a tall, stout man of about fifty, comely of feature but feeble of spirit. He looked anxious.

"Have they been? Are they gone? Have you got him? Is it over?" he asked rapidly.

"Not yet," returned Moore with phlegm. "We are waiting for them."

"They'll not come; it's near noon. Better give it up. It will excite bad feeling--make a stir--cause perhaps fatal consequences."

"_You_ need not appear," said Moore. "I shall meet them in the yard when they come; _you_ can stay here."

"But my name must be seen in the law proceedings. A wife and family, Mr. Moore--a wife and family make a man cautious."

Moore looked disgusted. "Give way, if you please," he said. "Leave me to myself. I have no objection to act alone; only be assured you will not find safety in submission. Your partner Pearson gave way, and conceded, and forbore. Well, that did not prevent them from attempting to shoot him in his own house."

"My dear sir, take a little wine and water," recommended Mr. Helstone.

The mixture was hollands and water, as Sykes discovered when he had compounded and swallowed a brimming tumbler thereof. It transfigured him in two minutes, bringing color back to his face and making him at least _word_-valiant. He now announced that he hoped he was above being trampled on by the common people; he was determined to endure the insolence of the working-classes no longer; he had considered of it, and made up his mind to go all lengths; if money and spirit could put down these rioters, they should be put down; Mr. Moore might do as he liked, but _he_—Christie Sykes—would spend his last penny in law before he would be beaten; he'd settle them, or he'd see.

"Take another glass," urged Moore.

Mr. Sykes didn't mind. This was a cold morning; it was necessary to be careful at this season of the year; it was proper to take something to keep the damp out; he had a little cough already; something of this sort was excellent, taken medicinally; he didn't make a practice of drinking spirits in a morning, but occasionally it really was prudent to take precautions.

"Quite prudent, and take them by all means," urged the host.

Mr. Sykes now addressed Mr. Helstone, who stood on the hearth, his shovel-hat on his head, watching him significantly with his little, keen eyes.

"You, sir, as a clergyman," said he, "may feel it disagreeable to be present amidst scenes of hurry and flurry, and, I may say, peril. I dare say your nerves won't stand it. You're a man of peace, sir; but we manufacturers, living in the world, and always in turmoil, get quite belligerent. Really, there's an ardour excited by the thoughts of danger that makes my heart pant. When Mrs. Sykes is afraid of the house being attacked and broke open--as she is every night--I get quite excited. I couldn't describe to you, sir, my feelings. Really, if anybody was to come--thieves or anything--I believe I should enjoy it, such is my spirit."

The hardest of laughs, though brief and low, and by no means insulting, was the response of the rector. Moore would have pressed upon the heroic mill-owner a third tumbler, but the clergyman, who never transgressed, nor would suffer others in his presence to transgress, the bounds of decorum, checked him.

"Enough is as good as a feast, is it not, Mr. Sykes?" he said; and Mr. Sykes assented, and then sat and watched Joe Scott remove the bottle at a sign from Helstone, with a self-satisfied simper on his lips and a regretful glisten in his eye. Moore looked as if he should have liked to fool him to the top of his bent. What would a certain young kinswoman of his have said could she have seen her dear, good, great Robert—her Coriolanus—just now? Would she have acknowledged in that mischievous, sardonic visage the same face to which she had looked up with such love, which had bent over her with such gentleness last night? Was that the man who had spent so quiet an evening with his sister and his cousin—so suave to one, so tender to the other—reading Shakespeare and listening to Chénier?

Yes, it was the same man, only seen on a different side—a side Caroline had not yet fairly beheld, though perhaps she had enough sagacity faintly to suspect its existence. Well, Caroline had, doubtless, her defective side too. She was human. She must, then, have been very imperfect; and had she seen Moore on his very worst side, she would probably have said this to herself and excused him. Love can excuse anything except meanness; but meanness kills love, cripples even natural affection; without esteem true love cannot exist. Moore, with all his faults, might be esteemed; for he had no moral scrofula in his mind, no hopeless polluting taint—such, for instance, as that of falsehood; neither was he the slave of his appetites. The active life to which he had been born and bred had given him something else to do than to join the futile chase of the pleasure-hunter. He was a man undegraded, the disciple of reason, _not_ the votary of sense. The same might be said of old Helstone. Neither of these two would look, think, or speak a lie; for neither of them had the wretched black bottle, which had just been put away, any charms. Both might boast a valid claim to the proud title of "lord of the creation," for no animal vice was lord of them; they looked and were superior beings to poor Sykes.

A sort of gathering and trampling sound was heard in the yard, and then a pause. Moore walked to the window; Helstone followed. Both stood on one side,

The atmosphere in the rectory office shifted instantly when Mr. Moore emerged from his private consultation. Messrs. Helstone and Sykes, emboldened by the dismissal of the workmen's deputation, greeted him with a chorus of jovial congratulations, praising his steely resolve. Yet, Mr. Moore remained unnervingly still. His face was a mask of somber calm, devoid of the warmth or animation expected of a man receiving such accolades. It was the visage of a stormless, cloud-choked sky—devoid of sun or wind. Mr. Helstone, perceiving the dissonance between his words and Moore's demeanor, wisely checked his flowery praise. He buttoned his coat, signaling the end of the performance, and suggested to Sykes that they depart, leaving Moore to his solitary reverie.

As they reached the door, Moore paused, his gaze fixed. "Where is Sugden?" he asked, his voice flat.

Helstone grinned, revealing a secret he had kept while Moore was occupied. "Ah, ha! I confess to a little unauthorized assistance. While you parleyed with that downcast gentleman, Farren, I slipped through the back window, summoned Murgatroyd, and smuggled Sugden and his brother Moses—wooden leg and all—into Sykes's gig. Sugden drove like a demon, and within fifteen minutes, Barraclough was safely en route to Stilbro' jail."

"Very good," Moore replied simply, offering a polite nod before ushering them out.

For the remainder of the day, Mr. Moore was a man of few words. Even Joe Scott, the miller's foreman, found him reticent. Joe, usually chatty, offered only the bare necessities of business, casting sidelong glances at his master. As the mill slowed for the evening, Joe ventured a suggestion: "It's a grand evening, sir. A walk up th' Hollow might do you good."

Moore burst into a short, sharp laugh. "Do you take me for a woman or a child, Joe?" he demanded, seizing the keys from the foreman's hand and shoving him toward the gate. He called Joe back, however, before he reached the yard.

"Do you know those Farrens?" Moore asked. "Are they well off?"

"They cannot be, sir," Joe replied, his voice dropping. "No work for three months. William is ruined, fair paired. They've sold most of their goods."

"He was a good workman?"

"The best you've had since you started."

"And decent folk?"

"Niver dacenter. The wife is a canting body, but clean enough to eat off the floor. They are down and out. William knows gardening; he learned it from a Scot. He could manage a garden."

"Then you may go."

Joe departed, leaving Moore alone in the silence of the counting-house.

Outside, the spring evening had turned raw. Though the day had been warm, the air had chilled at sunset, and a hoar frost was silently creeping over the grass and buds. The pavement in front of Briarmains, Mr. Yorke's residence, was already white. The great oak guarding the gable and the walnut grove behind the house stood defiant against the cold, their bare branches stark against the twilight.

Inside, however, life was vibrant. Lights burned brightly in the windows. Briarmains stood near the highway, close enough to hear the hum of Briarfield and see the glare of the new Wesleyan chapel, Briar Chapel, just a hundred yards away. Within its walls, a prayer meeting was in full swing. The hymns were raucous and ecstatic, sung with a fervor that seemed to defy the quiet countryside.

*"Oh! who can explain / This struggle for life... / Plague, earthquake, and famine... / The wonderful coming / Of Jesus declare!"*

The congregation moved from hymn to hymn with buoyant energy, interspersed with periods of clamorous prayer and fearful groans. Shouts of "I've found liberty!" rang out, followed by anthems of unspeakable bliss and the fathomless love of God. The climax arrived with a terrifying shout: *"Here we raise our voices higher, / Shout in the refiner's fire, / Clap our hands amidst the flame, / Glory give to Jesus' name!"* The roof remained intact, a testament to its sturdy slating.

Yet, if the chapel was alive with noise, Briarmains possessed a different kind of vitality. The lower windows opened onto the lawn, curtains drawn but not fully obscuring the candlelight within. Voices and laughter drifted out, hinting at a gathering of the family.

The scene inside the back parlour was intimate and charged with atmosphere. The room was decorated with stained glass windows featuring the heads of Shakespeare and Milton, alongside Canadian landscapes and a vivid painting of Vesuvius erupting. A fierce coal fire burned in the ample chimney, a luxury Mr. Yorke insisted upon regardless of the season.

Mr. Yorke sat beside the fire, not reading, but watching his children. Opposite him sat his wife, a large woman of grave aspect. Her face bore the weight of a perpetual, voluntary cloud of care. To her, mirth was profanity and cheerfulness frivolity. She was a stern, democratic woman with a cynical view of human nature, suspicious of all men and creeds. She was a devoted wife and mother, yet her suspicion extended to her husband's friendships, keeping his relations at arm's length.

Their children were a study in contrasts. The youngest, a baby, clung to her mother's knee, the only one she did not yet suspect or condemn. Next were the girls, Rose and Jessy. Rose, twelve, sat at her father's knee, resembling him in spirit but softer in feature. Her eyes held a serious, mature intelligence that frightened her father, who feared she might not live long. Jessy, the younger, was a chattering, arch creature, a pet to her father and a rival to her mother. She was destined for a short life, a fact foreshadowed by the narrator's vision of her grave in a foreign land, while Rose was envisioned as a lonely emigrant in the southern hemisphere.

The three eldest sons—Matthew, Mark, and Martin—sat in a corner playing a game. Matthew, the eldest, possessed a sinister intensity, his face reminiscent of the erupting volcano on the wall. He was the favored son, treated with a protective coddling that bred resentment in his brothers. Mark was calm and shrewd, his soul already middle-aged despite his youth. Martin, the youngest, was plain and cynical, a boy who vowed to hate women and wear only blue cloth, promising a future transformation into something handsome and brilliant.

A tap at the door announced the arrival of Robert Moore. Mrs. Yorke greeted him with her characteristic solemnity, questioning his nocturnal wandering. Moore, a man of gravity and abstemious habits, exchanged pleasantries, though Mrs. Yorke remained wary of his bachelor status.

"Well, is it you?" she asked as he approached. "You should be at home."

"Can a single man be said to have a home, madam?" Moore countered.

"Pooh!" she dismissed, her plain speaking often startling. "Pray, does not your sister make a home for you?"

"Not she," Mr. Yorke added dryly. "Hortense is an honest lass, but I looked out for a wife."

Mrs. Yorke cracked a dry joke about her own repentance of marriage, pointing to her brood of rough lads as evidence of her punishment. She warned Moore against the temptations of matrimony, citing their four sons and two daughters as a deterrent.

"I am not tempted now," Moore replied. "These are not times for marrying."

This lugubrious sentiment pleased Mrs. Yorke, who nodded in acquiescence. Soon, little Jessy jumped from her father's lap to run into Moore's arms.

"You talk of marrying him," she declared indignantly to her mother. "He promised that I should be his wife last summer! Didn't he, father?"

"Ay, my little lassie," Mr. Yorke confirmed. "But make him say it over again. Such as he are only false loons."

"He is not false. He is too bonny to be false," Jessy insisted, looking up at Moore with absolute trust.

"Bonny!" Mr. Yorke scoffed. "That's the reason he should be, and proof that he is, a scoundrel."

"But he looks too sorrowful to be false," Rose interjected quietly from behind her father.

"Your sentimental buck is the greatest cheat of all," Mr. Yorke remarked.

"He's not sentimental," Rose corrected.

Moore turned to her, surprised. "How do you know I am not sentimental, Rose?"

"Because I heard a lady say you were not."

"Voilà, qui devient intéressant!" Mr. Yorke exclaimed, drawing his chair closer to the fire. "A lady! We must guess who it is."

Rose refused to name her, describing the encounter instead. She recalled a party at Mrs. Pearson's where several ladies had discussed Moore. Some called him a misanthrope and a "solemn puppy." Others noted an affectation in his dark hair and pale face, comparing him to a "sentimental noodle."

"And who contradicted them?" Moore asked.

"A lady whom I don't know," Rose said. "She never visits here, though I see her every Sunday at church. She sits near the pulpit. She is like a picture in our dining-room, with a dove in her hand. She has eyes like it, and a straight nose that makes her face look clear."

Jessy, impatient with her sister's vagueness, revealed the identity. "She means Caroline Helstone, the rector's niece. Miss Helstone was quite angry with Anne Pearson. She said, 'Robert Moore is neither affected nor sentimental; you mistake his character utterly.'"

Jessy proceeded to describe Miss Helstone in detail: fair, with a slender white throat, long loose brown curls, and a quiet, clear tone. She often wore a gray silk dress and was neat in every way. "She is what I call a lady," Jessy declared. "When I am as tall as she is, I mean to be like her. Shall I suit you if I am? Will you really marry me?"

Moore stroked Jessy's hair but gently pushed her away. "Oh! you won't have me? You push me away."

"Why, Jessy, you care nothing about me," Moore said, feigning hurt. "You never come to see me now at the Hollow."

"Because you don't ask me," Jessy retorted.

Moore, seeing the opportunity, invited both girls to visit him the next day at the Hollow, promising presents. Just as Jessy was about to respond, Martin interjected cynically.

"I know that Miss Helstone you have all been palavering about. She's an ugly girl. I hate her. I hate all womenites. I wonder what they were made for."

"Martin!" Mr. Yorke warned.

Martin merely turned his truculent face toward his father. "I'll say the same then. I mean always to hate women. They're such dolls... I'll never marry. I'll be a bachelor."

Mr. Yorke chuckled, recalling his own youthful misogamy before he had traveled to France and Italy and become a lover of women. Martin, however, vowed to wear only blue cloth, rejecting the colorful attire of others as beneath human dignity.

Mark, observing the tension, stepped in with a slow, ironic voice. "Mr. Moore, you think perhaps it was a compliment on Miss Helstone's part to say you were not sentimental. I thought you appeared confused... as if you felt flattered."

Mark explained his definition: a sentimental man is one with thoughts and ideas; an unsentimental man is destitute of them.

"There are different kinds of thoughts, ideas, and notions," Rose interjected gently. "Sentimental must refer to the bad, or Miss Helstone must have taken it in that sense, for she was not blaming Mr. Moore; she was defending him."

"That's my kind little advocate!" Moore said, taking Rose's hand.

"She was defending him," Rose repeated, noting the spiteful tone of the other ladies.

"Ladies always do speak spitefully," Martin observed. "It is the nature of womenites to be spiteful."

Matthew, the eldest, finally spoke up. "What a fool Martin is, to be always gabbling about what he does not understand!"

"It is my privilege, as a freeman, to gabble on whatever subject I like," Martin retorted.

"You use it, or rather abuse it, to such an extent," Matthew rejoined, "that you prove you ought to have been a slave."

"A slave! a slave! That to a Yorke, and from a Yorke!" Martin stood up, pointing at Matthew. "This fellow forgets, what every cottier in Briarfield knows, that all born of our house have that arched instep under which water can flow—proof that there has not been a slave of the blood for three hundred years."

"Mountebank!" Matthew sneered.

"Lads, be silent!" Mr. Yorke commanded. "Martin, you are a mischief-maker."

"Indeed! Is that correct? Did I begin, or did Matthew?" Martin challenged.

"A presumptuous fool!" Matthew repeated.

Mrs. Yorke began to rock herself, a movement that often preceded hysterics when Matthew was defeated. "I don't see why I should bear insolence from Matthew Yorke," Martin protested.

"He has no right, my lad; but forgive your brother until seventy-and-seven times," Mr. Yorke soothed.

"Always alike, and theory and practice always adverse!" Martin murmured as he turned to leave.

"Where art thou going, my son?" his father asked.

"Somewhere where I shall be safe from insult, if in this house I can find any such place."

Matthew laughed insolently. Martin threw a strange look at him, trembling slightly, but restrained himself.

"I suppose there is

Spring arrived, painting England's surface in greens and blooms, yet the nation's heart remained heavy. The war dragged on, draining wealth and shedding blood for ends that seemed increasingly hollow. While sporadic victories echoed from the Peninsula, they were drowned out by the arrogant triumphalism of Bonaparte. For those suffering under the conflict, the struggle felt endless and desperate; merchants like Yorke and Moore, trembling on the brink of ruin, demanded peace with the frantic energy of those fighting for survival.

Meetings were held, petitions drafted, and speeches made, driven by a singular desire to escape the financial paralysis threatening the trading classes. Yet, in their collective selfishness, these men cared little for the terms of peace, only for the preservation of their own commerce. The narrative of the era painted a stark picture of a society ruled by Mammon, where chivalry had died and honor was secondary to profit. The British merchant, it was argued, would endure any insult from the French until the very last coin was threatened; only then would they transform into "British bulldogs," biting at the thief's throat until the treasure was restored. When speaking against war, they claimed moral superiority, yet their hearts were often cold, viewing anyone outside their trade as a parasite consuming the bread of idleness.

Robert Moore, though no self-sacrificing patriot, was acutely aware of his own precarious position. Urged toward ruin, he fought with vigor, stirring agitation in the north and leveraging his connections to influence others. Yet, amidst the chaos, flashes of reason pierced his despair. He saw Europe arming against Bonaparte, Russia rising to defend her frozen soil and serfs from foreign tyranny, and realized that a free England could not submit to such injustice. News of Lord Wellington's steady, unostentatious progress in the Peninsula offered a glimmer of hope—a power that was vigilant and genuine, destined eventually to win. But for Moore, that victory felt distant. In the meantime, he feared he would be crushed, his hopes ground to dust. His focus narrowed to his own survival and destiny.

This fierce determination led to a decisive rupture with his old Tory friend, the rector, Mr. Helstone. Their disagreement erupted at a public meeting and escalated into pungent newspaper exchanges. Mr. Helstone denounced Moore as a Jacobin, severed all ties, and explicitly ordered his niece, Caroline, to cease her visits to Hollow's Cottage and abandon her French lessons. He dismissed the language as frivolous and harmful to female minds, comparing it to feeding a rickety child on chalk and water. He viewed Moore's presence at the rectory with suspicion, fearing clandestine meetings.

Mr. Helstone was a man of sharp observation. He had noticed that while the boisterous Malone entertained the household with his antics, causing Caroline to vanish upstairs, Robert Moore's quiet, gentle demeanor drew her into a state of wondrous contentment. He had witnessed them exchange glances—once, only once—eyes that were clear and gentle in Moore, manly and searching in Caroline. Each acknowledged a charm in the other, a silent understanding that annoyed the rector, who viewed such flirtation as a folly to be chastised. Combining these private suspicions with his political objections, Helstone resolved to separate the cousins.

One evening, as Caroline worked near the window, Helstone delivered his command in a dry, significant tone. He noted her paleness and the absence of Robert's name from her lips for weeks, suspecting secret rendezvous. Caroline acquiesced immediately, her face devoid of the expected flush or tears. The shadow of thoughtfulness that had settled over her remained undisturbed. She obeyed because the mandate aligned with her own judgment; going to Hollow's Cottage had become a source of pain. Hope and love had departed that little tenement, replaced by the reality that Robert seemed to have deserted its precincts.

At church, the only remaining chance to see him, Caroline rarely looked. To do so was both too much pain and too much pleasure, an emotion she knew was wasted. On one dark, wet Sunday, however, she caught a glimpse of him alone in his pew. He looked sad and abstracted, his face a tintless olive, his thoughts wrapped in mysteries of business and responsibility that excluded her entirely. She meditated on his condition, realizing the vast difference between their lives. Her heart was consumed by love, a predominant emotion for two years, while his mind was occupied by forces she could not comprehend. He would not turn his head; he gave her no glance. The grief of being unseen choked her.

That evening, alone in her chamber with a cheerless fire, Caroline's mind wandered through images of shared winters and summers, of nuts and berries shared like a bird feeding its fledgling. But these visions faded, leaving the cold reality of her narrow room. She faced the terrifying prospect of her future: a life of seventy years without marriage or children. She mused on the role of the old maid, rejecting the notion that virtue lay solely in self-abnegation. She questioned the hollowness of an existence given away to others without something of one's own to bestow. Reflecting on mortality and the soul's journey, she found comfort only in the biblical promise of a refuge in eternity.

The following day, with her uncle away, Caroline spent her time in silent contemplation. Fanny, her maid, observed her mistress's stillness and urged her to visit Miss Mann or Miss Ainley, noting that old maids were often unhappy. Caroline, however, dismissed the idea of Miss Mann as a dismal prospect, fearing the "unhappy race" of old maids. Yet, Fanny's insistence sparked a resolve. Caroline decided to visit Miss Ainley, wondering what life had been to make her unamiable.

Upon arriving at Miss Mann's, Caroline found a scene of perfect, almost austere neatness. Miss Mann sat primly in a rocking chair, her gaze fixed with a formidable intensity that Robert Moore had once described as comparable to Medusa's. This stare, combined with a dry, monotonous voice, felt like a graven image of a bad spirit. However, as Caroline spoke with a sympathetic tone, the old maid's demeanor softened. Caroline saw the bloodless pallor and deeply worn lines of Miss Mann's face, recognizing the loneliness behind the grim exterior. Miss Mann, usually reserved, opened up, revealing a history of cruel, slow-wasting sufferings and devoted care for a wretched relative. Caroline realized that Miss Mann's moroseness was a shield against a world that had forgotten her, and she resolved to offer her affection and respect, visiting her weekly.

Next, Caroline visited Miss Ainley, a woman even plainer and poorer than Miss Mann. Despite her unattractive appearance and starched manners, Miss Ainley possessed a serene, unselfish mind. She spoke only of others, their wants and sufferings, displaying a religious devotion that commanded reverence. Her goodness was evident in her service to the poor, nursing the sick and working tirelessly despite her own privations. Though she received little reward in this life, her sincerity was undeniable. Caroline, who had previously dismissed such figures as objects of ridicule, now bowed her mind in reverence before Miss Ainley's practical excellence.

Caroline returned home with a new resolve. She planned to divide her time between study, charitable work directed by Miss Ainley, and exercise, aiming to stem the anguish that had poisoned her spirit. She determined to be wise if she could not be good, refusing to waste her youth in inert languor. Yet, despite her conscientious efforts, the change was not immediate. The work helped to keep her occupied and prevented brooding, offering fleeting moments of satisfaction when she did good. However, the deep ache of lost love persisted. Her memory kept harping on Robert Moore's name; a funereal cry haunted her, and the heaviness of a broken spirit settled slowly on her buoyant youth. Winter seemed to be conquering her spring, her mind's soil freezing into barren stagnation, even as she strove to build a life of purpose.

Caroline refused to yield. Within the quiet fortress of her girlhood lay a native strength, a resilience she summoned with every breath. It is a peculiar law of the human heart that when one struggles alone—without witnesses, counselors, or the comforting weight of shared burden—the fight becomes absolute. Miss Helstone was in this solitary siege. Her suffering was her only spur, and because it was so sharp, so visceral, it ignited a fierce determination within her. She threw herself into her duties with a frantic energy unseen before. She walked in every weather, taking long, solitary paths that wound through the dreary skirts of Stilbro' Moor or the sun-drenched expanses of Nunnely Common. Each evening she returned pale and worn, yet strangely unflagged. As soon as she shed her bonnet and shawl, she would not rest; instead, she would pace her room until she was nearly faint, convinced that only by exhausting herself during the day could she achieve a sound sleep at night. Yet this aim remained elusive. While others slept, she tossed in the darkness or sat at the foot of her couch, trapped in a cycle of intolerable despair that periodically reduced her to childlike helplessness.

In these moments of prostration, temptation whispered in her weary heart. The voice of weakness suggested writing to Robert, confessing her unhappiness at being forbidden to see him and Hortense, fearing he would withdraw his friendship—and perhaps his love—and forget her entirely. She drafted one or two such letters, her hand trembling, but shame and common sense tore them up before they could be sent.

Eventually, the pressure became unbearable. She felt her heart and mind straining toward breaking. A deep, secret yearning took root in her: the desire to leave Briarfield, to flee to some distant place where the past could not follow. Coupled with this was the anxious hope of discovering her mother, a figure shrouded in mystery. Yet, doubt clung to this hope like a shadow. Had she known her mother, could she love her? The memories were cold; no one had ever heard her mother praised. Her uncle regarded his sister-in-law with a tacit antipathy, and old servants spoke of her with chilling reserve, calling her "queer" or saying they did not understand her. These icy words suggested a terrifying possibility: that it might be better never to know her parent than to know her and find her unworthy.

With no other outlet, she framed a plan to become a governess. It was the only path open to her. A small incident finally gave her the courage to reveal this design to her uncle. Her nightly walks invariably led her near the Hollow. Though she rarely descended, she visited the brink of the den at twilight with the regularity of the rising stars. From a specific stile beneath an ancient thorn, she could survey the cottage, the mill, the dewy garden, and the deep dam. Most importantly, she could see the counting-house window. Her purpose was to watch for the sudden flare of the lamp within—a signal that promised connection. Sometimes the light sparkled bright in clear air; sometimes it shimmered dimly through mist; often it fractured between slanting lines of rain. She came in all weathers, driven by the hope that catching that light would mean seeing Robert.

On nights when the lamp failed to appear, she knew he was away, and her sorrow doubled. But when the light kindled, her spirit soared, as if she saw in it the promise of indefinite hope. If, while she gazed, a shadow bent between the light and the lattice, her heart leaped. That eclipse was Robert. She had seen him. She returned home comforted, carrying a clearer vision of his aspect, a distinct memory of his voice and smile. Blended with these impressions was a sweet persuasion that if she could get near him, his heart might still welcome her, that he might yet extend his hand and shelter her as he once did. That night, though she wept, her tears felt less scalding; the pillow seemed softer; the ache in her temples lessened.

The shortest path from the Hollow to the rectory wound near a certain mansion, the old and tenantless dwelling known as Fieldhead. Though vacant for ten years, it was not a ruin. Mr. Yorke had ensured it was kept in good repair, and an old gardener and his wife resided there, maintaining the grounds and the house. If Fieldhead lacked other merits, it was certainly picturesque. Its regular architecture, softened by time into gray and mossy hues, gave it a claim to beauty. The old latticed windows, the stone porch, the walls, the roof, and the chimney-stacks were rich in crayon touches and sepia shadows. The trees behind were fine and bold; the cedar on the lawn was grand; and the granite urns on the garden wall, along with the fretted arch of the gateway, were a delight to the eye.

One mild May evening, Caroline, passing near moonrise and feeling too weary yet unwilling to return to the bed of thorns and night of grief awaiting her, sat on the mossy ground near the gate. She gazed toward the cedar and the mansion. It was a still, calm, dewy, cloudless night. The gables, turned west, reflected the clear amber of the horizon; the oaks behind were black, the cedar blacker. Under its dense, raven boughs, a glimpse of sky opened, gravely blue, full of the moon, which looked solemnly and mildly down upon her from beneath that sombre canopy.

She felt the night and the prospect mournfully lovely. She wished for happiness, for inward peace, wondering if Providence had no pity for her. Recollections of happy trysts from old ballads returned to her mind; she imagined such a meeting in such a scene would be blissful. Where was Robert now? Not at the Hollow; she had watched for his lamp in vain. She questioned within herself whether she and Moore were ever destined to meet again. Suddenly, the door within the stone porch of the hall opened, and two men emerged—one elderly and white-headed, the other young, dark-haired, and tall. They crossed the lawn, passed through a portal in the garden wall, and disappeared down the fields. Robert Moore had passed before her with his friend Mr. Yorke. Neither had seen her.

The apparition was transient, scarce seen before it vanished, but its electric passage left her veins kindled and her soul insurgent. It found her despairing; it left her desperate.

"Oh, had he but been alone! Had he but seen me!" she cried in her heart. "He would have said something. He would have given me his hand. He *does*, he *must*, love me a little. He would have shown some token of affection. In his eye, on his lips, I should have read comfort; but the chance is lost. The wind, the cloud's shadow, does not pass more silently, more emptily than he. I have been mocked, and Heaven is cruel!"

Thus, in the utter sickness of longing and disappointment, she went home.

The next morning at breakfast, appearing white-cheeked and miserable as one who had seen a ghost, she inquired of Mr. Helstone, "Have you any objection, uncle, to my inquiring for a situation in a family?"

Her uncle, ignorant of the turmoil she underwent, scarcely believed his ears. "What whim now?" he asked. "Are you bewitched? What can you mean?"

"I am not well, and need a change," she said.

He examined her and discovered she had indeed experienced a change. Without his awareness, the rose had dwindled and faded to a mere snowdrop; bloom had vanished, flesh wasted. She sat before him drooping, colorless, and thin. But for the soft expression of her brown eyes, the delicate lines of her features, and the flowing abundance of her hair, she would no longer possess a claim to the epithet pretty.

"What on earth is the matter with you?" he asked. "What is wrong? How are you ailing?"

No answer came, only the brown eyes filling and the faintly-tinted lips trembling.

"Look out for a situation, indeed! For what situation are you fit? What have you been doing with yourself? You are not well."

"I should be well if I went from home."

"These women are incomprehensible. They have the strangest knack of startling you with unpleasant surprises. To-day you see them bouncing, buxom, red as cherries, and round as apples; to-morrow they exhibit themselves effete as dead weeds, blanched and broken down. And the reason of it all? That's the puzzle. She has her meals, her liberty, a good house to live in, and good clothes to wear, as usual. A while since that sufficed to keep her handsome and cheery, and there she sits now a poor, little, pale, puling chit enough. Provoking! Then comes the question, What is to be done? I suppose I must send for advice. Will you have a doctor, child?"

"No, uncle, I don't want one. A doctor could do me no good. I merely want change of air and scene."

"Well, if that be the caprice, it shall be gratified. You shall go to a watering-place. I don't mind the expense. Fanny shall accompany you."

"But, uncle, some day I must do something for myself; I have no fortune. I had better begin now."

"While I live, you shall not turn out as a governess, Caroline. I will not have it said that my niece is a governess."

"But the later in life one makes a change of that sort, uncle, the more difficult and painful it is. I should wish to get accustomed to the yoke before any habits of ease and independence are formed."

"I beg you will not harass me, Caroline. I mean to provide for you. I have always meant to provide for you. I will purchase an annuity. Bless me! I am but fifty-five; my health and constitution are excellent. There is plenty of time to save and take measures. Don't make yourself anxious respecting the future. Is that what frets you?"

"No, uncle; but I long for a change."

He laughed. "There speaks the woman!" cried he, "the very woman! A change! a change! Always fantastical and whimsical! Well, it's in her sex."

"But it is not fantasy and whim, uncle."

"What is it then?"

"Necessity, I think. I feel weaker than formerly. I believe I should have more to do."

"Admirable! She feels weak, and *therefore* she should be set to hard labour—*'clair comme le jour,'* as Moore—confound Moore! You shall go to Cliff Bridge; and there are two guineas to buy a new frock. Come, Cary, never fear. We'll find balm in Gilead."

"Uncle, I wish you were less generous and more----"

"More what?"

Sympathizing was the word on Caroline's lips, but she checked herself in time. Her uncle would indeed have laughed if that namby-pamby word had escaped her. Finding her silent, he said, "The fact is, you don't know precisely what you want."

"Only to be a governess."

"Pooh! mere nonsense! I'll not hear of governessing. Don't mention it again. It is rather too feminine a fancy. I have finished breakfast. Ring the bell. Put all crotchets out of your head, and run away and amuse yourself."

"What with? My doll?" asked Caroline to herself as she quitted the room.

A week or two passed; her bodily and mental health neither grew worse nor better. She was now precisely in that state when, if her constitution had contained the seeds of consumption, decline, or slow fever, those diseases would have been rapidly developed, and would soon have carried her quietly from the world. People never die of love or grief alone, though some die of inherent maladies which the tortures of those passions prematurely force into destructive action. The sound by nature undergoes these tortures, and are racked, shaken, shattered; their beauty and bloom perish, but life remains untouched. They are brought to a certain point of dilapidation; they are reduced to pallor, debility, and emaciation. People think, as they see them gliding languidly about, that they will soon withdraw to sick-beds, perish there, and cease from among the healthy and happy. This does not happen. They live on; and though they cannot regain youth and gaiety, they may regain strength and serenity. The blossom which the March wind nips, but fails to sweep away, may survive to hang a withered apple on the tree late into autumn: having braved the last frosts of spring, it may also brave the first of winter.

Every one noticed the change in Miss Helstone's appearance, and most people said she was going to die. She never thought so herself. She felt in no dying case; she had neither pain nor sickness. Her appetite was diminished; she knew the reason. It was because she wept so much at night. Her strength was lessened; she could account for it. Sleep was coy and hard to be won; dreams were distressing and baleful. In the far future she still seemed to anticipate a time when this passage of misery should be got over, and when she should once more be calm, though perhaps never again happy.

Meanwhile her uncle urged her to visit, to comply with the frequent invitations of their acquaintance. This she evaded doing. She could not be cheerful in company; she felt she was observed there with more curiosity than sympathy. Old ladies were always offering her their advice, recommending this or that nostrum; young ladies looked at her in a way she understood, and from which she shrank. Their eyes said they knew she had been "disappointed," as custom phrases it; by whom, they were not certain.

Commonplace young ladies can be quite as hard as commonplace young gentlemen—quite as worldly and selfish. Those who suffer should always avoid them. Grief and calamity they despise; they seem to regard them as the judgments of God on the lowly. With them, to "love" is merely to contrive a scheme for achieving a good match; to be "dis

Shirley proved her sincerity in desiring Caroline's company by actively seeking it, for Miss Helstone was naturally reticent about forming new acquaintances. Burdened by the belief that she was unwanted and incapable of entertaining, the shy governess viewed the vibrant, wealthy heiress of Fieldhead as too self-sufficient to appreciate her own modest society. Yet, Shirley, though brilliant and likely happy, found that no one exists entirely independent of warm social interaction. Within a month, she had cultivated friendly relations with the Sykes sisters, the Pearsons, and the formidable Misses Wynne of Walden Hall, yet she confessed to Mrs. Pryor that she felt no genuine geniality among them. She did not fraternize with any of them. Shirley mused that if she were truly the Earl of Briarfield, she would not wish to marry a single woman in the neighboring parishes, a sentiment Mrs. Pryor received with quiet amusement, warning her against adopting masculine mannerisms.

Shirley never mocked her former governess, respecting even her formalities as signs of quiet worth. Standing by the window, watching a bird on a grand cedar tree, Shirley began to chirrup, then whistle, executing a tune with surprising skill. When Mrs. Pryor protested, Shirley admitted she had forgotten her resolution not to whistle before her. Explaining that she had learned the habit by listening to a gentleman whistling in the fields, she identified him as Mr. Moore, the only non-gray-haired gentleman in the region, contrasting him favorably with the venerable Mr. Helstone and Mr. Yorke.

Mrs. Pryor remained silent until Shirley proposed taking Caroline for a walk on Nunnely Common. Persuading the diffident Caroline to join her, Shirley led her onto the quiet road. Once the initial shyness faded, Caroline found conversation with Miss Keeldar refreshing. Shirley spoke of the heath-covered ridges, recalling a sultry journey across Scottish moors where the landscape turned a livid purple-black, edged with brass and white, threatening with lightning. Caroline described the scene vividly, noting how the hills seemed rolled in a sullen mist before being washed from the world by rain. They paused on the green brow of the common to gaze down at Nunnely Dale, a valley robed in May's green, where the shadow of a cloud rested on Nunnwood—the last remnant of ancient British forest.

Shirley declared Yorkshire one of England's bonniest nooks, revealing she was born there, with five generations of her family sleeping in Briarfield Church. Caroline shook her hand, declaring them compatriots. Shirley described Nunnwood as an encampment of forest giants, a place of olden silence and ruin, specifically pointing out a dell containing the remains of a nunnery. She proposed an excursion there, alone with Caroline, to sketch and enjoy the solitude. Caroline eagerly agreed, listing her knowledge of the wood's hidden glades, mosses, and ancient trees, insisting that the presence of gentlemen would destroy the charm of such a retreat.

Reflecting on the nature of companionship, Caroline noted that while fools irritated, clever men caused a different, sharper pain: the insecurity of one's own worthiness in their company. Shirley dismissed this fear, asserting her confidence in associating with the best men. The conversation drifted to Caroline's uncle, the rector. Shirley praised his sensible face, but Caroline revealed a stark contrast: he was charming in society but stern and silent at home, locking his warmth away in his study. She described him as liberal rather than good-natured, brilliant rather than genial, and scrupulously equitable rather than truly just.

This led to a profound discussion on marriage. Shirley confessed that the thought of becoming a burden to a husband terrified her; she preferred the freedom to withdraw into solitude when her company was no longer welcome. Caroline agreed, citing her uncle's cynical view that marriage was a burden and that men inevitably grow sick of their wives. Shirley countered that they usually find an exception in the one they love, trusting in his kindness to animals and children as proof of his future fidelity. However, Caroline recalled her uncle's prediction that any man would be sick of her in a month.

Shirley then spoke of Robert Moore with enthusiasm, praising his handsome features and princely forehead. Caroline confirmed his nobility, adding that he possessed a mind that made him the lord of creation. They discussed the nature of true poetry, agreeing that it must stem from real feeling rather than intellectual display. Reciting Cowper's "The Castaway," they found solace in the poet's ability to transform anguish into art. Shirley noted that one could love Cowper only to comfort him, while Caroline insisted he was a nature too elevated for human love, comparing him to Rousseau—a being of clay and gold, repulsive in totality despite divine sparks.

The conversation turned to Caroline's relationship with Robert. Shirley questioned why he had cut a lock of her hair if he was so indifferent. Caroline admitted it was her own doing; she had asked for it, and he had traded a lock of his for one of hers. She confessed to feeling foolish and despising herself for this small, sharp recollection that lacerated her self-respect. Just as Shirley urged her to stop dwelling on her weaknesses, a tap at the door interrupted them. Shirley received a note from Mr. Moore. She declined to open it immediately, summoning candles instead, while Caroline waited expectantly. Soon after, the rector's niece, Fanny, arrived and departed, leaving the two women alone once more.

There was a peculiar, gentle lethargy in Shirley's nature. At certain intervals, she found profound joy in absolute stillness—moments when her hands and eyes rested, and her simple existence, surrounded by the world and beneath the heavens, yielded such a fullness of happiness that she felt no need to move a single finger to increase it. Often, following an active morning, she would spend a sun-drenched afternoon lying motionless on the grass at the foot of a shady tree. She required no company save that of Caroline, provided she was nearby; she asked for no spectacle save the deep blue sky and the drifting clouds; she listened only to the hum of bees and the whisper of leaves. In such hours, her only books were the dim chronicle of memory or the prophetic page of anticipation. A glorious light seemed to emanate from her young eyes as she read these volumes, and a smile played upon her lips, revealing glimpses of the tales or prophecies within. It was not a sad or dark smile. Fate had been kind to this blissful dreamer, promising favor anew. Her past held sweet passages, and her future brimmed with rosy hopes.

Yet, one day, when Caroline approached to rouse her, believing she had lain long enough, she beheld, to her surprise, that Shirley's cheek was wet as if with dew; those fine eyes shone humid and brimming with tears.

"Shirley, why do *you* cry?" Caroline asked, involuntarily stressing the pronoun.

Miss Keeldar smiled and turned her picturesque head toward the questioner. "Because it pleases me mightily to cry," she said. "My heart is both sad and glad. But why, you good, patient child—why do you not bear me company? I only weep tears, delightful and soon wiped away; you might weep gall, if you choose."

"Why should I weep gall?"

"Mateless, solitary bird!" was the only answer.

"And are not you too mateless, Shirley?"

"At heart—no."

"Oh! who nestles there, Shirley?"

But Shirley only laughed gaily at this question and alertly started up.

"I have dreamed," she said, "a mere day-dream—certainly bright, probably baseless!"

***

By this time, Miss Helstone was largely free from illusions. She took a sufficiently grave view of the future and fancied she knew pretty well how her own destiny and that of others were tending. Yet, old associations retained their influence over her, and it was these, combined with the power of habit, that still frequently drew her in the evenings to the field-stile and the old thorn overlooking the Hollow.

One night, the night after the incident of the note, she was at her usual post, watching for her beacon—watching in vain: that evening no lamp was lit. She waited until the rising of certain constellations warned her of lateness and signaled her departure. In passing Fieldhead on her return, its moonlit beauty attracted her glance and stayed her step for an instant. Tree and hall rose peacefully under the night sky and the clear, full orb; pearly paleness gilded the building; mellow brown gloom bosomed it round; shadows of deep green brooded above its oak-wreathed roof. The broad pavement in front shone pale also; it gleamed as if some spell had transformed the dark granite to glistering Parian. On the silvery space slept two sable shadows, thrown sharply defined from two human figures. These figures, when first seen, were motionless and mute; presently they moved in harmonious step and spoke in a low, harmonious key.

Earnest was the gaze that scrutinized them as they emerged from behind the trunk of the cedar. "Is it Mrs. Pryor and Shirley?"

Certainly it is Shirley. Who else has a shape so lithe, proud, and graceful? And her face, too, is visible—her countenance careless and pensive, musing and mirthful, mocking and tender. Not fearing the dew, she has not covered her head; her curls are free, veiling her neck and caressing her shoulder with their tendril rings. An ornament of gold gleams through the half-closed folds of the scarf she has wrapped across her bust, and a large bright gem glitters on the white hand which confines it. Yes, that is Shirley.

Her companion is, of course, Mrs. Pryor?

Yes, if Mrs. Pryor owns six feet of stature, and if she has changed her decent widow's weeds for a masculine disguise. The figure walking at Miss Keeldar's side is a man—a tall, young, stately man; it is her tenant, Robert Moore.

The pair speak softly; their words are not distinguishable. To remain a moment to gaze is not to be an eavesdropper; and as the moon shines so clearly and their countenances are so distinctly apparent, who can resist the attraction of such interest? Caroline, it seems, cannot, for she lingers.

There was a time when, on summer nights, Moore had been wont to walk with his cousin, as he was now walking with the heiress. Often had she gone up the Hollow with him after sunset, to scent the freshness of the earth, where a growth of fragrant herbage carpeted a certain narrow terrace edging a deep ravine. From the rifted gloom of that ravine was heard a sound like the spirit of the lonely watercourse, moaning amongst its wet stones, between its weedy banks, and under its dark bower of alders.

"But I used to be closer to him," thought Caroline. "He felt no obligation to treat me with homage; I needed only kindness. He used to hold my hand; he does not touch hers. And yet Shirley is not proud where she loves. There is no haughtiness in her aspect now, only a little in her port—what is natural to and inseparable from her, what she retains in her most careless as in her most guarded moments. Robert must think, as I think, that he is at this instant looking down on a fine face; and he must think it with a man's brain, not with mine. She has such generous yet soft fire in her eyes. She smiles—what makes her smile so sweet? I saw that Robert felt its beauty, and he must have felt it with his man's heart, not with my dim woman's perceptions. They look to me like two great happy spirits. Yonder silvered pavement reminds me of that white shore we believe to be beyond the death-flood. They have reached it; they walk there united. And what am I, standing here in shadow, shrinking into concealment, my mind darker than my hiding-place? I am one of this world, no spirit—a poor doomed mortal, who asks, in ignorance and hopelessness, wherefore she was born, to what end she lives; whose mind forever runs on the question, how she shall at last encounter, and by whom be sustained through death.

"This is the worst passage I have come to yet; still I was quite prepared for it. I gave Robert up, and gave him up to Shirley, the first day I heard she was come, the first moment I saw her—rich, youthful, and lovely. She has him now. He is her lover. She is his darling. She will be far more his darling yet when they are married. The more Robert knows of Shirley, the more his soul will cleave to her. They will both be happy, and I do not grudge them their bliss; but I groan under my own misery. Some of my suffering is very acute. Truly I ought not to have been born; they should have smothered me at the first cry."

Here, Shirley stepping aside to gather a dewy flower, she and her companion turned into a path that lay nearer the gate. Some of their conversation became audible. Caroline would not stay to listen. She passed away noiselessly, and the moonlight kissed the wall which her shadow had dimmed. The reader is privileged to remain, and try what he can make of the discourse.

"I cannot conceive why nature did not give you a bulldog's head, for you have all a bulldog's tenacity," said Shirley.

"Not a flattering idea. Am I so ignoble?"

"And something also you have of the same animal's silent ways of going about its work. You give no warning; you come noiselessly behind, seize fast, and hold on."

"This is guess-work. You have witnessed no such feat on my part. In your presence I have been no bulldog."

"Your very silence indicates your race. How little you talk in general, yet how deeply you scheme! You are far-seeing; you are calculating."

"I know the ways of these people. I have gathered information of their intentions. My note last night informed you that Barraclough's trial had ended in his conviction and sentence to transportation. His associates will plot vengeance. I shall lay my plans so as to counteract or at least be prepared for theirs—that is all. Having now given you as clear an explanation as I can, am I to understand that for what I propose doing I have your approbation?"

"I shall stand by you so long as you remain on the defensive. Yes."

"Good! Without any aid—even opposed or disapproved by you—I believe I should have acted precisely as I now intend to act, but in another spirit. I now feel satisfied. On the whole, I relish the position."

"I dare say you do. That is evident. You relish the work which lies before you still better than you would relish the execution of a government order for army-cloth."

"I certainly feel it congenial."

"So would old Helstone. It is true there is a shade of difference in your motives—many shades, perhaps. Shall I speak to Mr. Helstone? I will, if you like."

"Act as you please. Your judgment, Miss Keeldar, will guide you accurately. I could rely on it myself in a more difficult crisis. But I should inform you Mr. Helstone is somewhat prejudiced against me at present."

"I am aware—I have heard all about your differences. Depend upon it, they will melt away. He cannot resist the temptation of an alliance under present circumstances."

"I should be glad to have him; he is of true metal."

"I think so also."

"An old blade, and rusty somewhat, but the edge and temper still excellent."

"Well, you shall have him, Mr. Moore—that is, if I can win him."

"Whom can you not win?"

"Perhaps not the rector; but I will make the effort."

"Effort! He will yield for a word—a smile."

"By no means. It will cost me several cups of tea, some toast and cake, and an ample measure of remonstrances, expostulations, and persuasions. It grows rather chill."

"I perceive you shiver. Am I acting wrongly to detain you here? Yet it is so calm—I even feel it warm—and society such as yours is a pleasure to me so rare. If you were wrapped in a thicker shawl—"

"I might stay longer, and forget how late it is, which would chagrin Mrs. Pryor. We keep early and regular hours at Fieldhead, Mr. Moore; and so, I am sure, does your sister at the cottage."

"Yes; but Hortense and I have an understanding the most convenient in the world, that we shall each do as we please."

"How do you please to do?"

"Three nights in the week I sleep in the mill—but I require little rest—and when it is moonlight and mild I often haunt the Hollow till daybreak."

"When I was a very little girl, Mr. Moore, my nurse used to tell me tales of fairies being seen in that Hollow. That was before my father built the mill, when it was a perfectly solitary ravine. You will be falling under enchantment."

"I fear it is done," said Moore, in a low voice.

"But there are worse things than fairies to be guarded against," pursued Miss Keeldar.

"Things more perilous," he subjoined.

"Far more so. For instance, how would you like to meet Michael Hartley, that mad Calvinist and Jacobin weaver? They say he is addicted to poaching, and often goes abroad at night with his gun."

"I have already had the luck to meet him. We held a long argument together one night. A strange little incident it was; I liked it."

"Liked it? I admire your taste! Michael is not sane. Where did you meet him?"

"In the deepest, shadiest spot in the glen, where the water runs low, under brushwood. We sat down near that plank bridge. It was moonlight, but clouded, and very windy. We had a talk."

"On politics?"

"And religion. I think the moon was at the full, and Michael was as near crazed as possible. He uttered strange blasphemy in his Antinomian fashion."

"Excuse me, but I think you must have been nearly as mad as he, to sit listening to him."

"There is a wild interest in his ravings. The man would be half a poet, if he were not wholly a maniac; and perhaps a prophet, if he were not a profligate. He solemnly informed me that hell was foreordained my inevitable portion; that he read the mark of the beast on my brow; that I had been an outcast from the beginning. God's vengeance, he said, was preparing for me, and affirmed that in a vision of the night he had beheld the manner and the instrument of my doom. I wanted to know further, but he left me with these words, 'The end is not yet.'"

"Have you ever seen him since?"

"About a month afterwards, in returning from market, I encountered him and Moses Barraclough, both in an advanced stage of inebriation. They were praying in a frantic sort at the roadside. They accosted me as Satan, bid me avaunt, and clamoured to be delivered from temptation. Again, but a few days ago, Michael took the trouble of appearing at the counting-house door, hatless, in his shirt-sleeves—his coat and castor having been detained at the public-house in pledge.

The morning light filtered through the window, illuminating the turmoil within Shirley's mind. As she rose, her first words were a declaration of inevitability: "Of course, I know he will marry Shirley." She spoke with a firm conviction that he ought to, for she believed Shirley possessed the strength to aid him. Yet, beneath this rational acceptance lay a cruel, gnawing thought: *I shall be forgotten.* The image of a future where Robert belonged entirely to another woman, where she would be erased from his life, sent a chill through her. *What shall I do?* she wondered, clutching the thought of her own Robert. She felt unworthy of such a title, viewing herself as poverty and incapacity compared to Shirley's wealth, power, and radiant beauty. There was no flaw in Shirley's claim; she loved him with a depth that commanded pride. Shirley was the rightful choice.

Consequently, Shirley resolved to accept this union. But she drew a hard line in the sand regarding her own future role. She despised the hollow pretense of being a sister or a distant friend. To a man like Robert, she demanded everything or nothing. She would not endure the degradation of feigning calm affection while her soul tore itself apart. Once the couple was united, she vowed to leave them, refusing to linger as a hypocrite or a nuisance. She could not stand between them, nor could she trample them. The alternative was clear: she must either cleave to him as part of his very being or be severed from him as widely as the poles of a sphere. "Sunder me then, Providence," she whispered to the empty room, praying for a swift separation.

These dark aspirations resurfaced late in the afternoon when Miss Keeldar appeared at the window, her usual blend of wistfulness and careless gaiety visible even in her slow saunter. Inside, she greeted Caroline with a sharp inquiry about her broken promise to visit.

"I was not in the humour," Caroline admitted truthfully.
