Shirley's gaze was immediate and piercing. "No," she observed, her tone shifting from curiosity to a pitying intensity. "You are not in the humour for loving me. You are in one of your sunless moods, when a fellow creature's presence feels like a burden. You have such moods. Are you aware of it?"

"Do you mean to stay long, Shirley?" Caroline asked, sensing the edge in her friend's voice.

"Yes. I am here for tea, and I shall take the liberty of removing my bonnet." She did so, standing on the rug with her hands clasped behind her back, her expression unreadable yet heavy with unspoken sorrow.

"You look wonderfully self-supported," Shirley continued, her eyes scanning Caroline's face. "Like a wounded deer seeking solitude. Are you afraid I will worry if I discover you are hurt?"

"I never fear Shirley," Caroline replied.

"But sometimes you dislike her; often you avoid her. Shirley can feel when she is slighted. If you had not walked home with Moore last night, you would be a different girl today. What time did you reach the rectory?"

"By ten."

Shirley frowned. "Three-quarters of an hour to walk a mile? Was it you, or Moore, who lingered?"

"Shirley, you talk nonsense."

"He talked nonsense," Shirley countered, her irritation flaring. "Or he looked it, which is far worse. I see the reflection of his eyes on your forehead right now. I feel disposed to call him out, if only I had a trustworthy second. I am desperately irritated. I felt so last night, and I have carried it all day."

"You don't ask me why," Shirley pressed, her voice dropping. "You, the silent, over-modest thing, don't deserve my secrets poured into your lap without an invitation. Upon my word, I could have found it in my heart to dog Moore yesterday evening with dire intent. I have pistols, and I can use them."

"Stuff, Shirley! Which would you have shot—me or Robert?"

"Neither, perhaps. Perhaps myself. More likely a bat or a tree-bough. He is a puppy, your cousin—a quiet, serious, sensible, ambitious puppy. I see him standing before me, bearing me down with his fixity of purpose, and I have no patience with him!"

Miss Keeldar paced the room, venting her frustration against men in general and her tenant in particular.

"You are mistaken," Caroline urged, anxiety tightening her chest. "Robert is no puppy or male flirt. I can vouch for that."

"_You_ vouch for it! Do you think I'll take your word? There is no testimony I would credit sooner than yours. To advance Moore's fortune, you would cut off your right hand."

"But I didn't lie. He was just civil to me last night—that was all."

"I never asked what he was. I can guess. I saw him from the window take your hand in his long fingers just as he left my gate."

"That is nothing. I am an old acquaintance, his cousin."

"I feel indignant," Shirley declared, her voice rising. "All my comfort is broken up by his manoeuvres. He keeps intruding between you and me. Without him, we should be good friends; but that six feet of puppyhood creates a perpetual eclipse of our friendship. He crosses and obscures the disc I want to see clear; he renders me to you a mere bore and nuisance."

"No, Shirley, no."

"He does. You did not want my society this afternoon, and I feel it hard. I am a social personage who cannot live alone. If we were left unmolested, I could bear you in my presence forever. You cannot say as much respecting me."

"Shirley, I can say anything you wish. I like you."

"You will wish me at Jericho tomorrow, Lina."

"I shall not. I am growing fonder of you every day. You are too English to form a vehement friendship instantly, but you are so different from every-day young ladies. I esteem you, I value you. You are never a burden. Do you believe what I say?"

"Partly," Shirley replied, smiling with a touch of incredulity. "But you are a peculiar personage. Quiet as you look, there is force and depth within you that is not easily reached. Then you certainly are not happy."

"And unhappy people are rarely good. Is that what you mean?"

"Not at all. I mean that unhappy people are often preoccupied, unable to discourse with companions of my nature. Moreover, there is a sort of unhappiness that corrodes. That, I fear, is your portion. Will pity do you any good, Lina? If it will, take some from Shirley; she offers it largely, and warrants it genuine."

"Shirley, I never had a sister—you never had a sister—but it flashes on me now how sisters feel towards each other. Affection twined with their lives, which no shock can uproot, which little quarrels only trample momentarily before springing fresher. Affection that no passion can ultimately outrival, with which even love itself can only compete in force and truth. Love hurts us, Shirley. It is tormenting, racking, burning away our strength. In affection, there is no pain, no fire, only sustenance and balm. I am supported and soothed when you are near. Only you. Do you believe me now?"

"I am always easy of belief when the creed pleases me. We really are friends, then, Lina, in spite of the black eclipse?"

"We really are," Caroline returned, drawing Shirley down to sit beside her. "Chance what may."

"Then, let us talk of something else than the Troubler."

Just then, the rector entered, and the conversation shifted. When Miss Keeldar finally prepared to depart, she paused in the passage to confide a heavy secret. "Caroline, I have a great weight on my mind. My conscience is uneasy, as if I had committed a crime. Not my private conscience, but my conscience as a landed proprietor and lord of the manor. I have fallen under the clutch of an eagle with iron talons. I am under a stern influence I scarcely approve but cannot resist. Something terrible will happen ere long, and I fear it. To ease my mind and prevent harm, I intend to embark on a series of good works. Do not be surprised if I suddenly become outrageously charitable. I have no idea how to begin, but you must advise me. Tomorrow, ask Miss Ainley to come to Fieldhead. I intend to put myself under her tuition. Won't she have a precious pupil? Hint to her, Lina, that though well-meaning, I am a neglected character, and she will feel less scandalized by my ignorance regarding clothing societies."

The next morning, Caroline found Shirley sitting gravely at her desk, surrounded by an account book, a bundle of banknotes, and a well-filled purse. She looked serious, yet puzzled. "I have been casting an eye over the weekly expenditure at the hall," she explained, "trying to find where I could retrench. I also gave audience to Mrs. Gill, the cook, and sent her away convinced my brain was crazed. I lectured her on economy in a way quite new to her. I was so eloquent on the text of thrift that I surprised myself. It is a fresh idea; I never thought or spoke on it until lately. But it is all theory. When I came to the practical part, I could retrench nothing. I lacked the firmness to remove a single pound of butter or to investigate the destiny of dripping, lard, or bread. We do not wash for the parish, yet I viewed in silence items of soap and bleaching powder calculated to satisfy the most anxious inquirer. I am not carnivorous, nor is Mrs. Pryor, nor Mrs. Gill, yet I only hemmed and widened my eyes when I saw butcher's bills proving a falsehood. Caroline, you may laugh at me, but you cannot change me. I am a poltroon on certain points. I feel it. There is a base alloy of moral cowardice in my composition. I blushed and hung my head before Mrs. Gill when she ought to have been faltering confessions to me. I found it impossible to summon the spirit to hint, much less prove, that she was a cheat. I have no calm dignity, no true courage."

"Shirley, what fit of self-injustice is this?" Caroline protested. "My uncle, who is not given to speak well of women, says there are not ten thousand men in England as genuinely fearless as you."

"I am fearless, physically. I am never nervous about danger. I was not startled when Mr. Wynne's great red bull rose with a bellow before my face, stooping his begrimed head to charge me. But I was afraid of seeing Mrs. Gill brought to shame. You have twice, ten times, my strength of mind on certain subjects. You, whom no persuasion can induce to pass a bull, would have firmly shown my housekeeper she had done wrong, then gently admonished her, and sweetly forgiven her if she seemed penitent. Of this conduct, I am incapable."

"However," Shirley continued, "in spite of exaggerated imposition, I still find we live within our means. I have money in hand, and I must do some good with it. The Briarfield poor are badly off. They must be helped. What ought I to do, Lina? Should I not distribute the cash at once?"

"No, indeed, Shirley. You will not manage properly. I have noticed that your notion of charity is to give shillings and half-crowns in a careless, free-handed way, liable to abuse. You need a prime minister, or you will get into scrapes. You suggested Miss Ainley yourself; I will apply to her. Promise to keep quiet and stop throwing away your money. What a great deal you have! You must feel very rich?"

"Yes; I feel of consequence. It is not an immense sum, but I feel responsible for its disposal. This responsibility weighs on my mind more heavily than I expected. They say families are almost starving in Briarfield. Some of my own cottagers are in wretched circumstances. I must and will help them."

"Some people say we shouldn't give alms to the poor, Shirley."

"They are great fools for their pains. For those who are not hungry, it is easy to palaver about the degradation of charity. But they forget the brevity of life and its bitterness. We have none of us long to live. Let us help each other through seasons of want and woe without heeding the scruples of vain philosophy."

"But you do help others, Shirley. You give a great deal as it is."

"Not enough. I must give more, or my brother's blood will cry to Heaven against me. If political incendiaries come to kindle conflagration here and attack my property, I shall defend it like a tigress. I know I shall. Let me listen to Mercy as long as she is near me. Once her voice is drowned by the shout of ruffian defiance, I shall be full of impulses to resist. If the poor gather and rise as a mob, I shall turn against them as an aristocrat; if they bully me, I must defy; if they attack, I must resist."

"You talk like Robert."

"I feel like Robert, only more fiercely. Let them meddle with Robert, or his mill, or his interests, and I shall hate them. At present, I am no patrician, nor do I regard the poor as plebeians. But if they violently wrong me or mine and then presume to dictate to us, I shall forget pity for their wretchedness and respect for their poverty in scorn of their ignorance and wrath at their insolence."

"Shirley, how your eyes flash!"

"Because my soul burns. Would you, any more than me, let Robert be borne down by numbers?"

"If I had your power to aid Robert, I would use it as you mean to use it. If I could be such a friend to him as you can be, I would stand by him, as you mean to stand by him, till death."

"And now, Lina, though your eyes don't flash, they glow. You drop your lids, but I

The following morning, Shirley turned to Caroline with a radiant expression, recounting how thoroughly the gathering had succeeded.

"I truly enjoy hosting a circle of gentlemen," she confessed. "There is a peculiar amusement in observing how they delight in a meal carefully prepared for them. For us, these fine wines and scientifically arranged dishes hold little significance; yet gentlemen seem to retain a childlike simplicity regarding food. One delights in pleasing them—provided, of course, they display the dignified self-restraint of our worthy rectors. I often watch Moore, trying to discern what might please him, but he lacks that innocent simplicity. Have you ever discovered his weak point, Caroline? You have seen more of him than I."

"It is certainly not the same with my uncle or Dr. Boultby," Caroline replied, smiling. She always felt a shy pleasure in following Miss Keeldar's lead when discussing her cousin's character. Left to her own devices, she would never have broached the subject, but when invited, the temptation to speak of the man she constantly thought of was irresistible. "However," she added, "I confess I do not know what it is. I have never watched Robert without my scrutiny being baffled by the discovery that he was watching me."

"There it is!" Shirley exclaimed. "You cannot fix your gaze upon him without his flashing back at you. He is never off his guard; he refuses to give you the advantage. Even when he does not look at you, his thoughts seem busy among your own, tracing your words and actions to their source, contemplating your motives with perfect ease. Oh! I know that sort of character, or something akin to it. It piques me singularly. How does it affect you?"

This question exemplified one of Shirley's sharp, sudden turns. Caroline, initially fluttered by such remarks, had now learned to parry them with the steady composure of a little Quakeress.

"Pique you? In what way?" she asked.

"Here he comes!" Shirley suddenly cried, breaking off and darting to the window. "Here comes a diversion. I never told you of a superb conquest I have recently made—at those parties to which I can never persuade you to accompany me—and the feat was accomplished without effort or intention on my part, I swear. There is the bell! And, by all that is delicious, there are two of them. Do they never hunt except in pairs? You may have one, Lina, and you may choose. I hope I am generous enough. Listen to Tartar!"

The black-muzzled, tawny dog, previously glimpsed in the chapter introducing his mistress, let out a formidable bark from the hall, where the sound resonated deeply. This was followed by a growl more terrible than the bark itself, menacing like muttered thunder.

"Listen!" Shirley cried again, laughing. "You would think this the prelude to a bloody onslaught. They will be frightened. They do not know old Tartar as I do. They are unaware that his uproars are merely sound and fury, signifying nothing!"

Bustle erupted within. "Down, sir, down!" came a high-toned, imperious voice, accompanied by the crack of a cane. Immediately followed a yell, a scuttle, a run—a positive tumult.

"Oh, Malone, Malone!"

"Down! Down! Down!" the high voice commanded.

"He is really worrying them!" Shirley exclaimed. "They have struck him. A blow is something he is not accustomed to and will not tolerate."

She ran out. A gentleman fled up the oak staircase, seeking refuge in the gallery or upper chambers with hot haste; another backed rapidly toward the stairfoot, wildly flourishing a knotty stick while reiterating, "Down! Down! Down!" Meanwhile, the tawny dog bayed, bellowed, and howled, while a group of servants bundled out from the kitchen. The dog made a spring; the second gentleman turned tail and rushed after his companion. The first was already safe in a bedroom, holding the door shut against his fellow—nothing so merciless as terror. But the other fugitive struggled hard; the door was about to yield to his strength.

"Gentlemen," Shirley said in her silvery but vibrating tones, "spare my locks, if you please. Calm yourselves! Come down! Look at Tartar; he won't harm a cat."

She was caressing the beast. He lay crouched at her feet, forepaws stretched out, tail still agitated, nostrils snorting, his bulldog eyes conscious of a dull fire. He was an honest, phlegmatic, stupid, yet stubborn canine. He loved his mistress and John—the man who fed him—but was mostly indifferent to the rest of the world. Quiet enough unless struck or threatened with a stick, which instantly unleashed a demon within him.

"Mr. Malone, how do you do?" Shirley continued, lifting her mirth-lit face to the gallery. "That is not the way to the oak parlour; that is Mrs. Pryor's apartment. Request your friend Mr. Donne to evacuate. I shall have the greatest pleasure in receiving him in a lower room."

"Ha! Ha!" cried Malone in hollow laughter, quitting the door and leaning over the massive balustrade. "Really, that animal alarmed Donne. He is a little timid," he proceeded, stiffening himself and walking trimly to the stairhead. "I thought it better to follow in order to reassure him."

"It appears you did. Well, come down, if you please." Turning to her manservant, she said, "John, go upstairs and liberate Mr. Donne. Take care, Mr. Malone; the stairs are slippery."

In truth, they were, being of polished oak. The caution came a little late for Malone. He had already slipped in his stately descent, saved only by a clutch at the banisters that made the whole structure creak again.

Tartar seemed to think the visitor's descent was effected with unwarranted *éclat*, and accordingly he growled once more. Malone, however, was no coward. The spring of the dog had taken him by surprise, but he passed him now in suppressed fury rather than fear. If a look could have strangled Tartar, he would have breathed no more. Forgetting politeness in his sullen rage, Malone pushed into the parlour before Miss Keeldar. He glanced at Miss Helstone; he could scarcely bring himself to bend to her. He glared at both ladies. He looked as if, had either of them been his wife, he would have made a glorious husband at that very moment. In each hand, he seemed ready to clutch and gripe them to death.

However, Shirley took pity. She ceased to laugh; and Caroline, a true lady, did not smile even at anyone under mortification. Tartar was dismissed; Peter Augustus was soothed—for Shirley possessed looks and tones that could calm even a bull. He had sense enough to feel that, since he could not challenge the owner of the dog, he had better be civil. And civil he tried to be; and his attempts being well received, he grew presently very civil and quite himself again. He had indeed come for the express purpose of making himself charming and fascinating. Rough portents had met him on his first admission to Fieldhead, but that passage was over. Charming and fascinating he resolved to be. Like March, having come in like a lion, he purposed to go out like a lamb.

For the sake of air, or perhaps for a ready exit in case of some new emergency, he took his seat—not on the sofa where Miss Keeldar offered him enthronization, nor near the fireside to which Caroline, by a friendly sign, gently invited him, but on a chair close to the door. Being no longer sullen or furious, he grew, after his fashion, constrained and embarrassed. He talked to the ladies by fits and starts, choosing for topics whatever was most intensely commonplace. He sighed deeply, significantly, at the close of every sentence; he sighed in each pause; he sighed ere he opened his mouth. At last, finding it desirable to add ease to his other charms, he drew forth to aid him an ample silk pocket-handkerchief. This was to be the graceful toy with which his unoccupied hands were to trifle. He went to work with a certain energy. He folded the red-and-yellow square cornerwise; he whipped it open with a waft; again he folded it in narrower compass; he made of it a handsome band. To what purpose would he proceed to apply the ligature? Would he wrap it about his throat—his head? Should it be a comforter or a turban? Neither. Peter Augustus had an inventive, an original genius. He was about to show the ladies graces of action possessing at least the charm of novelty. He sat on the chair with his athletic Irish legs crossed, and these legs, in that attitude, he circled with the bandana and bound firmly together. It was evident he felt this device to be worth an encore; he repeated it more than once. The second performance sent Shirley to the window to laugh her silent but irrepressible laugh unseen; it turned Caroline's head aside, that her long curls might screen the smile mantling on her features. Miss Helstone, indeed, was amused by more than one point in Peter's demeanour. She was edified at the complete though abrupt diversion of his homage from herself to the heiress. The £5,000 he supposed her likely one day to inherit were not to be weighed in the balance against Miss Keeldar's estate and hall. He took no pains to conceal his calculations and tactics. He pretended to no gradual change of views; he wheeled about at once. The pursuit of the lesser fortune was openly relinquished for that of the greater. On what grounds he expected to succeed in his chase himself best knew; certainly not by skilful management.

From the length of time that elapsed, it appeared that John had some difficulty in persuading Mr. Donne to descend. At length, however, that gentleman appeared; nor, as he presented himself at the oak-parlour door, did he seem in the slightest degree ashamed or confused—not a whit. Donne, indeed, was of that coldly phlegmatic, immovably complacent, densely self-satisfied nature which is insensible to shame. He had never blushed in his life; no humiliation could abash him; his nerves were not capable of sensation enough to stir his life and make colour mount to his cheek; he had no fire in his blood and no modesty in his soul; he was a frontless, arrogant, decorous slip of the commonplace—conceited, inane, insipid; and this gentleman had a notion of wooing Miss Keeldar! He knew no more, however, how to set about the business than if he had been an image carved in wood. He had no idea of a taste to be pleased, a heart to be reached in courtship. His notion was, when he should have formally visited her a few times, to write a letter proposing marriage. Then he calculated she would accept him for love of his office; then they would be married; then he should be master of Fieldhead; and he should live very comfortably, have servants at his command, eat and drink of the best, and be a great man. You would not have suspected his intentions when he addressed his intended bride in an impertinent, injured tone—"A very dangerous dog that, Miss Keeldar. I wonder you should keep such an animal."

"Do you, Mr. Donne? Perhaps you will wonder more when I tell you I am very fond of him."

"I should say you are not serious in the assertion. Can't fancy a lady fond of that brute—'tis so ugly—a mere carter's dog. Pray hang him."

"Hang what I am fond of!"

"And purchase in his stead some sweetly pooty pug or poodle—something appropriate to the fair sex. Ladies generally like lap-dogs."

"Perhaps I am an exception."

"Oh, you can't be, you know. All ladies are alike in those matters. That is universally allowed."

"Tartar frightened you terribly, Mr. Donne. I hope you won't take any harm."

"That I shall, no doubt. He gave me a turn I shall not soon forget. When I *sor* him" (such was Mr. Donne's pronunciation) "about to spring, I thought I should have fainted."

"Perhaps you did faint in the bedroom; you were a long time there."

"No; I bore up that I might hold the door fast. I was determined not to let any one enter. I thought I would keep a barrier between me and the enemy."

"But what if your friend Mr. Malone had been worried?"

"Malone must take care of himself. Your man persuaded me to come out at last by saying the dog was chained up in his kennel. If I had not been assured of this, I would have remained all day in the chamber. But what is that? I declare the man has told a falsehood! The dog is there!"

And indeed Tartar walked past the glass door opening to the garden, stiff, tawny, and black-muzzled as ever. He still seemed in bad humour. He was growling again, and whistling a half-strangled whistle, being an inheritance from the bulldog side of his ancestry.

"There are other visitors coming," observed Shirley, with that provoking coolness which the owners of formidable-looking dogs are apt to show while their animals are all bristle and bay. Tartar sprang down the pavement towards the gate, bellowing *avec explosion*. His mistress quietly opened the glass door, and stepped out chirruping to him. His bellow was already silenced, and he was lifting up his huge, blunt, stupid head to the new callers to be patted.

"What! Tartar, Tartar!" said a cheery, rather boyish voice, "don't you know us? Good-morning, old boy!"

And little Mr. Sweeting, whose conscious good nature made him comparatively fearless of man, woman, child, or brute, came through the gate, caressing the guardian. His vicar, Mr. Hall, followed. He had no fear of Tartar either, and Tartar had no ill-will to him. He snuffed both the gentlemen round, and then, as if

The fund was thriving. Through Miss Keeldar's example, the rectors' tireless efforts, and the quiet, efficient support of their spinster lieutenants, Mary Ann Ainley and Margaret Hall, a substantial sum had been raised. Managed with prudence, it was already alleviating the suffering of the unemployed poor. The neighborhood seemed to exhale; for two weeks, no cloth had been burned, no mill or mansion desecrated. Shirley felt the storm passing overhead, convinced that with the arrival of summer, trade would inevitably revive. Peace must return, and with it, a surge for commerce.

She often voiced this optimism to her tenant, Gérard Moore, whenever they crossed paths. Moore listened with a silence that frustrated her, prompting impatient glances for an explanation. Yet, he would smile with that peculiar sweetness that softened his mouth while his brow remained grave. "I, too," he would reply, "trust in the war's finite nature. It is my anchor. I work Hollow's Mill entirely on speculation; I sell nothing, for there is no market. I manufacture for a future day. Three months ago, this was impossible—I had exhausted my credit and capital. You know who rescued me. On the strength of that loan, I continue this bold game. Total ruin awaits loss, and gain is doubtful, yet I remain cheerful. As long as my hands are free to strive, I cannot be depressed. One year—nay, six months—of the 'reign of the olive,' and I am safe. Peace will indeed impulse commerce, as you say. But regarding the tranquility your fund has brought, I harbor doubts. Alms never truly tranquilize the working classes; they do not make them grateful. It is not human nature. They feel the humiliation of needing relief, just as we would. To whom should they be grateful? To you, perhaps to the clergy, but certainly not to us mill-owners. They hate us more than ever. Furthermore, the disaffected here correspond with leaders in Nottingham, Manchester, and Birmingham. They are disciplined; no blow is struck without deliberation. Like the sky threatening thunder that clears only to gather again, the danger is merely delayed. The storm is sure to break eventually. There is an analogy between the moral and physical atmosphere."

"Well, Mr. Moore," she would conclude, "take care of yourself. If I have done you any good, reward me by promising to do the same."

"I will," he replied, his eyes distant. "I wish to live, not to die. The future opens like Eden before me, yet within that paradise, I see a vision I prefer to any seraph."

"Do you? Pray, what vision?"

"I see—"

A maid bustled in with the tea service, breaking the spell.

Early May had been fine, the middle wet, but the last week, with the change of the moon, had cleared. A fresh wind swept away the silver-white rain-clouds, carrying them eastward until the vault above was pure blue, ready for the summer sun. That sun rose broad and golden on Whitsuntide. The gathering of the schools was marked by splendid weather.

Whit-Tuesday was the great day. The two large schoolrooms of Briarfield, built by the present rector at great personal expense, had been scrubbed, whitewashed, and adorned with flowers and evergreens—some from the rectory garden, two cartloads from Fieldhead, and a wheelbarrow's worth from the stingy domain of Mr. Wynne. Twenty tables, each seating twenty guests, were laid out with white cloths, surrounded by benches. Suspended above were at least twenty cages containing canaries, a fancy cherished by Mr. Helstone's clerk who delighted in their piercing song, knowing they carolled loudest amidst confusion of tongues. These tables were not for the twelve hundred scholars, whose feast would be in the open air, but for the patrons and teachers. At one o'clock the troops would arrive; at two, they would be marshalled; by four, they would parade the parish, followed by the feast, music, and speeches in the church.

Briarfield was chosen not for size or antiquity—Whinbury was larger, Nunnely older—but simply because Mr. Helstone willed it so, and his will was stronger than the objections of Boultby or Hall. They let him lead.

This anniversary had always been a trial for Caroline Helstone, dragging her into public view where she faced the wealthy and influential without her mother or aunt. She trembled at the prospect, a nervous soul fearing publicity. But this year, Shirley was with her, and the trial vanished, transformed into an enjoyment. Miss Keeldar was better than a host of ordinary friends; self-possessed, spirited, and easy, she commanded courage merely by her presence. The only fear was Shirley's punctuality. The heiress often lingered, and Caroline knew her uncle would not wait. At the moment the church clock tolled two, the bells would clash and the march begin. Caroline resolved to keep watch on her companion.

Whit-Tuesday saw Caroline rise almost with the sun. She and her maids spent the morning arranging the rectory parlours and setting out a collation of wine, fruit, and cakes. Then came the dressing. She wore her freshest white muslin, a costume the solemnity of the occasion exacted. Her new sash—a birthday gift from Margaret Hall, likely purchased by Cyril himself in exchange for a set of cambric bands—was tied by Fanny's dexterous fingers. Her simple bonnet was trimmed to match, and her white crape scarf suited her perfectly. She formed a picture not bright enough to dazzle, but fair enough to interest; sweetness of tint and grace of mien atoned for the lack of rich color. Her brown eyes and clear forehead revealed a modest, gentle, and harmonious mind. Neither lamb nor dove would fear her.

Yet, as Cyril Hall noted, she was an imperfect human being, fair in form but not so good or great as the withered Miss Ainley, who was currently putting on her best black gown and Quaker drab shawl in her narrow cottage chamber.

Caroline departed across sequestered fields and hidden lanes to Fieldhead. She glided under green hedges and across leas, her white garment unstained by dust or moisture. After the rains, everything was clean and dry under the glowing sun. She walked fearlessly through daisies and thick plantations until she reached Miss Keeldar's dressing room.

It was fortunate she had come, for Shirley would have been late. Instead of hurrying, the heiress lay on a couch, absorbed in a book. Mrs. Pryor urged her to rise in vain. Caroline wasted no words; she took the book and began the business of disrobing and rerobing Shirley herself. Indolent with the heat and gay with her youth, Shirley wanted to talk and linger, but Caroline persevered, fastening strings and inserting pins with rapid efficiency. Finally, as she united the last row of hooks, she chided Shirley for her unpunctuality. Shirley did look the picture of incorrigible carelessness, yet a very lovely one.

There was a striking contrast between them. Shirley possessed style in every fold of her dress; the rich silk suited her better than simplicity, and the deep embroidered scarf became her. Worn negligently but gracefully, it complemented the wreath on her bonnet. Her attention to fashion matched the frank light in her eyes and the rallying smile about her lips. Caroline took her hand, hurried her downstairs, and they sped through the fields, laughing, resembling a snow-white dove and a gem-tinted bird of paradise in social flight.

Thanks to Miss Helstone's promptitude, they arrived just in time. Before the trees hid the church, they heard the urgent summons of the bell. The trampling of feet and murmuring of voices grew louder. From a rising ground, they saw the Whinbury school approaching on the road, numbering five hundred souls. The rector and curate, Boultby and Donne, headed it. The doctor loomed large in full canonicals, walking with the dignity of a beneficed priest under a shovel-hat, flourishing his cane and inclining it dogmatically toward his aide-de-camp, Mr. Donne. Though narrow compared to his principal, Donne contrived to look every inch a curate, from his turned-up nose to his square-toed shoes.

*Walk on, Mr. Donne!* Caroline thought. *You have undergone scrutiny. Whether the white and purple figures watching you from the hill approve is another question.*

These figures ran down as the regiment passed. The churchyard was full of children and teachers in their finest holiday attire. Despite the district's distress, it was wonderful to see how respectably they had dressed; the British love of decency worked miracles. Poverty could reduce an Irish girl to rags but could not rob the English girl of the neat wardrobe necessary for self-respect. Besides, the lady of the manor, Shirley, gazed with pleasure on the well-dressed crowd. Her bounty had consoled many families and supplied children with new frocks. She knew it and was elated, glad that her money and influence had substantially benefited those around her. She could not be charitable like Miss Ainley; it was not in her nature. It relieved her to know there was another way, practicable for her character.

Caroline, too, was pleased. She had sacrificed dresses and ribbons to fit out her scholars and, unable to give money, had followed Miss Ainley's example by sewing for them.

Not only was the churchyard full, but the rectory garden was thronged with ladies and gentlemen walking among lilacs and laburnums. The house was occupied; gay groups stood at the open windows. In the parson's croft, the musicians of the three parish bands awaited. Fanny and Eliza, in smart caps and aprons, moved among them serving quarts of ale, brewed sound and strong by the rector's special orders. Whatever he touched must be managed handsomely; "shabby doings" were not endured under his sanction. Miss Keeldar shared this sentiment, and they mutually approved each other's arrangements.

Soon Caroline and Shirley were in the midst of the company. Caroline, usually retiring to a corner, moved through the parlours, conversing and smiling, seemingly a new creature. It was Shirley's presence that transformed her; the heiress's air did her a world of good. Shirley had no fear of people; unless positively offensive, she welcomed everyone, treating them kindly until proven otherwise. This disposition made her a general favorite, robbing her raillery of sting and giving her conversation a happy charm. Her intimate friendship with Caroline was distinct from this social benevolence; Caroline was the choice of her affection and intellect, while others were merely profiteers by her good nature.

Mr. Donne entered the drawing room just as Shirley sat on the sofa, center of a circle. She had forgotten her exasperation against him and bowed good-humoredly. Yet his disposition was evident: he knew neither how to decline with dignified pride nor how to meet with frank forgiveness. His punishment had impressed him with no shame. He passed by sheepishly with a scowling look. Nothing could reconcile him to his enemy, yet no passion of resentment could his lymphatic nature know.

"He was not worth a scene!" Shirley whispered to Caroline. "What a fool I was! To revenge on poor Donne his silly spite at Yorkshire is like crushing a gnat for attacking the hide of a rhinoceros. Had I been a gentleman, I should have helped him off by force. I am glad I only employed the moral weapon. But he must come near me no more. He irritates me. There is no amusement in him. Malone is better sport."

Malone seemed to wish to justify the preference. As Shirley spoke, Peter Augustus approached, all in *grande tenue*, gloved, scented, and with hair oiled to perfection. He bore a huge bunch of cabbage-roses, five or six in full bloom. He presented them to the heiress with a grace that defied the pencil. Who could dare say Peter was not a lady's man? He had gathered and given flowers, offering a poetic tribute at the shrine of Love. Hercules holding the distaff was but a faint type of Peter bearing the roses. Amazed at his own gallantry, he backed away with a husky chuckle of self-satisfaction, then stopped to verify by ocular testimony that he had indeed presented the bouquet.

Yes, there were the six red cabbages on the purple satin lap, held by a white hand with gold rings, while streaming ringlets half hid a laughing face drooping over them. Only *half* hiding! Peter saw the laugh; it was unmistakable. He was made a joke of; his chivalry was the subject of jest for two petticoats—Miss Helstone too was smiling. He felt seen through, and Peter grew black as a thunder-cloud. When Shirley looked up, a fell eye was fastened on her. Malone, at least, had energy enough in hate. She saw it in his glance.

"Peter *is* worth a scene, and shall have it, if he likes, one day," she whispered.

Then, solemn and sombre in color though bland in face, the three rectors appeared at the dining-room door. Having finished their duties in the church, they came for refreshment before the march. The large morocco-covered chair was left vacant for Dr. Boultby. Caroline, obeying Shirley's instigation to play hostess, hastened to serve her uncle's vast, revered friend with wine and macaroons. His churchwardens, patrons of the Sunday school, were beside him; Mrs. Sykes and other ladies flanked him, expressing hopes he was not fatigued and fears the heat was too great. Mrs. Boultby, believing her lord's face became angelic after a good dinner, bent over him, tenderly wiping perspiration from his brow. Boultby was in his glory, rumbling out thanks in a round, sound voice. He took no notice of Caroline except to accept her offerings, seeing only the macaroons, which he possessed of in a small handful. The wine Mrs. Boultby insisted on mixing with hot water, sugar, and nutmeg.

Mr. Hall stood near an open window, breathing the fresh air and

This was no army marching to war, nor a band seeking enemies; yet the music they carried was martial, and the eyes of some—Miss Keeldar among them—betrayed a spirit stirred by the sound, though not by the thought of blood. Old Helstone glanced at her, and they exchanged a laugh.

"There is no battle in prospect," he said gently. "Our country does not ask us to fight. There is no tyrant threatening our liberty. We are merely taking a walk. Keep your hand on the reins, Captain, and dampen that fire. It is not needed."

"Take your own advice, Doctor," Shirley replied. To Caroline, she murmured, "I shall borrow from imagination what reality refuses. We are not soldiers; bloodshed is not my desire. If we are soldiers, we are soldiers of the Cross. Time seems to have rolled back centuries, binding us on a pilgrimage to Palestine. But no, that is too visionary. I crave a sterner dream. We are Lowlanders of Scotland, following a Covenanting captain into the hills to meet out of reach of persecuting troopers. We know battle may follow prayer, and that in the worst outcome, heaven must be our reward. We are ready to redden the peat-moss with our blood. That music stirs my soul; it wakes all my life, making my heart beat not with its usual pulse, but with a thrilling, new vigour. I almost long for danger—for a faith, a land, or at least a lover to defend."

"Look, Shirley!" Caroline interrupted. "What is that red speck above Stilbro' Brow? You have keener sight than I. Turn your eagle eye to it."

Miss Keeldar looked. "I see," she said, then added quickly, "there is a line of red. They are cavalry soldiers. They ride fast. There are six of them. They will pass us. No; they have turned right. They saw our procession and are avoiding it. Where are they going?"

"Perhaps they are only exercising their horses."

"Perhaps so. We see them no more."

Mr. Helstone spoke up. "We shall pass through Royd Lane to reach Nunnely Common by a short cut," he said.

Into the straits of Royd Lane they defiled. It was so narrow that only two could walk abreast without falling into the ditches lining the sides. Halfway through, excitement rippled through the clerical commanders. Boultby's spectacles trembled; Helstone's cane shook; the curates nudged one another nervously, while Mr. Hall turned to the ladies with a smile.

"What is the matter?" someone demanded.

He pointed his staff toward the end of the lane. There, emerging from the shadows, was another procession, led by men in black, accompanied by music.

"Is it our double?" Shirley asked, her voice tight. "Our manifold wraith? Here is a card turned up."

"If you wanted a battle, you are likely to get one—at least of looks," Caroline whispered, laughing.

"They shall not pass us!" the curates cried in unison. "We will not give way!"

"Give way!" Helstone retorted sternly, turning around. "Who speaks of giving way? You, boys, mind your business. The ladies, I know, will be firm. I can trust them. There is not a churchwoman here who will not stand her ground against these folks for the honour of the Establishment. What does Miss Keeldar say?"

"She asks what it is."

"The Dissenting and Methodist schools, the Baptists, Independents, and Wesleyans," Helstone declared, "have joined in an unholy alliance, turning purposely into this lane to obstruct our march and drive us back."

"Bad manners!" Shirley exclaimed. "And I hate bad manners. Of course, they must have a lesson."

"A lesson in politeness," Mr. Hall suggested, ever the peacemaker. "Not an example of rudeness."

Old Helstone quickened his pace, marching yards ahead of his company. When he neared the opposing leaders—a large, greasy man with black hair combed flat on his forehead—the hostile commander called a halt. The procession paused. He drew a hymn book, gave out a verse, set a tune, and they launched into the most dolorous of canticles.

Helstone signaled his bands. They clashed out with all the power of brass. He ordered "Rule, Britannia!" and commanded the children to join vocally, which they did with enthusiastic spirit. The enemy was sung and stormed down; his psalm was quelled. By noise alone, he was conquered.

"Now, follow me!" Helstone exclaimed. "Not at a run, but at a firm, smart pace. Be steady, every child and woman. Keep together. Hold on by each other's skirts if necessary."

He strode on with a determined, deliberate gait, perfectly seconded by his scholars and teachers. Neither ran nor faltered; they marched with cool, solid impetus. The curates, caught between Helstone and Miss Keeldar—who watched any deviation with lynx-eyed vigilance, ready to rebuke the slightest breach of order with cane or parasol—were compelled to match their step. First amazed, then alarmed, the body of Dissenters was borne down, pressed back, and finally forced to turn tail, leaving the outlet from Royd Lane free. Boultby suffered in the onslaught, but Helstone and Malone held him up, bringing him through the business whole in limb, though sorely tried in wind.

The fat Dissenter who had sung the hymn was left sitting in the ditch. A spirit merchant by trade and a leader of the Nonconformists, it was said he had drunk more water in that single afternoon than he had swallowed for a year prior. Mr. Hall had ensured Caroline's safety, and Caroline had cared for him. Later, he and Miss Ainley made quiet comments on the incident. When the party had fairly cleared the lane, Miss Keeldar and Mr. Helstone shook hands heartily. The curates began to exult, but Helstone soon put a curb on their innocent spirits, reminding them that the business was none of their managing and that they had better hold their tongues.

About half-past three, the procession turned back, regaining the starting place by four. Long lines of benches were arranged in the close-shorn fields surrounding the school. Children were seated, and huge baskets covered with white cloths, along with great smoking tin vessels, were brought out. Before the distribution of good things began, a brief grace was pronounced by Mr. Hall and sung by the children. Their young voices sounded melodious, even touching, in the open air. Large currant buns and hot, well-sweetened tea were administered in the proper spirit of liberality. No stinting was permitted; the rule was that each child should receive about twice as much as they could possibly eat, leaving a reserve for those prevented from coming by age, sickness, or other impediments. Buns and beer circulated among the musicians and singers. Afterwards, the benches were removed, and they were left to unbend their spirits in licensed play.

A bell summoned the teachers, patrons, and patronesses to the schoolroom. Miss Keeldar, Miss Helstone, and many other ladies were already there, glancing over the arrangement of their separate trays and tables. Most of the female servants of the neighbourhood, along with the clerks', singers', and musicians' wives, had been pressed into service as waiters. Each vied with the other in smartness and daintiness of dress, and many handsome forms were seen among the younger ones. About half a dozen were cutting bread and butter, another half-dozen supplying hot water from the rector's kitchen coppers. The profusion of flowers and evergreens decorating the white walls, the show of silver teapots and bright porcelain on the tables, and the active figures in blithe faces and gay dresses flitting about everywhere formed a refreshing and lively spectacle. Everybody talked, not very loudly, but merrily, while canary birds sang shrilly in their high-hung cages.

As the rector's niece, Caroline took her place at one of the three first tables; Mrs. Boultby and Margaret Hall officiated at the others. At these tables, the *élite* of the company were to be entertained, strict rules of equality not being more in fashion at Briarfield than elsewhere. Miss Helstone removed her bonnet and scarf to escape the oppressive heat. Her long curls, falling on her neck, served almost in place of a veil, and her muslin dress was fashioned modestly as a nun's robe, enabling her to dispense with the encumbrance of a shawl.

The room was filling. Mr. Hall had taken his post beside Caroline, who, as she rearranged cups and spoons, whispered remarks on the day's events. He looked grave about what had transpired in Royd Lane, and she tried to smile him out of his seriousness. Miss Keeldar sat near—unusually still, neither laughing nor talking. She seemed afraid lest some intruder should take a seat she apparently wished to reserve next to her own. Ever and anon she spread her satin dress over an undue portion of the bench, or laid her gloves or embroidered handkerchief upon it. Caroline noticed this *manège* and asked what friend she expected. Shirley bent toward her, almost touching her ear with rosy lips, and whispered with a musical softness that often characterized her tones when stirring some sweet secret source of feeling, "I expect Mr. Moore. I saw him last night and made him promise to come with his sister, and to sit at our table. He won't fail me, I feel certain; but I apprehend his coming too late, and being separated from us. Here is a fresh batch arriving; every place will be taken. Provoking!"

In fact, Mr. Wynne the magistrate, his wife, his son, and his two daughters now entered in high state. They were Briarfield gentry. Of course, their place was at the first table, and being conducted thither, they filled up the whole remaining space. For Miss Keeldar's comfort, Mr. Sam Wynne inducted himself into the very vacancy she had kept for Moore, planting himself solidly on her gown, her gloves, and her handkerchief. Mr. Sam was one of the objects of her aversion, and the more so because he showed serious symptoms of an aim at her hand. The old gentleman had also publicly declared that the Fieldfield and De Walden estates were delightfully *contagious*—a malapropism which rumour had not failed to repeat to Shirley.

Caroline's ears still rang with that thrilling whisper, "I expect Mr. Moore"; her heart still beat and her cheek still glowed with it, when a note from the organ pealed above the confused hum of the place. Dr. Boultby, Mr. Helstone, and Mr. Hall rose, as did all present, and grace was sung to the accompaniment of the music; then tea began. She was kept too busy with her office for a while to have leisure for looking round, but when the last cup was filled, she threw a restless glance over the room. There were some ladies and several gentlemen standing about, unaccommodated with seats. Amidst a group, she recognized her spinster friend, Miss Mann, whom the fine weather had tempted, or some urgent friend had persuaded, to leave her drear solitude for one hour of social enjoyment. Miss Mann looked tired of standing; a lady in a yellow bonnet brought her a chair. Caroline knew well that *chapeau en satin jaune*; she knew the black hair, and the kindly though rather opinionated and froward-looking face under it; she knew that *robe de soie noire*, she knew even that *schall gris de lin*; she knew, in short, Hortense Moore, and wanted to jump up and run to her—to give her one embrace for her own sake and two for her brother's. She half rose, indeed, with a smothered exclamation, and perhaps, for the impulse was very strong, she would have run across the room and actually saluted her; but a hand replaced her in her seat, and a voice behind her whispered, "Wait till after tea, Lina, and then I'll bring her to you."

And when she could look up, there was Robert himself close behind, smiling at her eagerness, looking better than she had ever seen him look—looking, indeed, to her partial eyes, so very handsome that she dared not trust herself to hazard a second glance; for his image struck on her vision with painful brightness, and pictured itself on her memory as vividly as if daguerreotyped by a pencil of keen lightning.

He moved on and spoke to Miss Keeldar. Shirley, irritated by some unwelcome attentions from Sam Wynne and by the fact of that gentleman being still seated on her gloves and handkerchief—and probably also by Moore's want of punctuality—was by no means in good humour. She first shrugged her shoulders at him, and then said a bitter word or two about his "insupportable tardiness." Moore neither apologized nor retorted. He stood near her quietly, as if waiting to see whether she would recover her temper; which she did in little more than three minutes, indicating the change by offering him her hand. Moore took it with a smile, half-corrective, half-grateful. The slightest possible shake of the head delicately marked the former quality; it is probable a gentle pressure indicated the latter.

"You may sit where you can now, Mr. Moore," said Shirley, also smiling. "You see there is not an inch of room for you here; but I discern plenty of space at Mrs. Boultby's table, between Miss Armitage and Miss Birtwhistle. Go! John Sykes will be your *vis-à-vis*, and you will sit with your back towards us."

Moore, however, preferred lingering about where he was. He now and then took a turn down the long room, pausing to interchange greetings with other gentlemen in his own placeless predicament; but still he came back to the magnet, Shirley, bringing with him, each time he returned, observations it was necessary to whisper in her ear.

Meantime, poor Sam Wynne

The evening air hung heavy and still, a sultry warmth promising a stifling night. Around the sinking sun, clouds blazed with a purple fire, casting Indian-like summer hues across the horizon. These rosy reflections bathed the hillsides, the fronts of houses, the trunks of trees, the winding roads, and the rolling pastures in a soft, dreamlike glow. The two girls descended slowly from the fields. By the time they reached the churchyard, the bells had fallen silent; the crowds had retreated inside. The entire scene was solitary.

"How peaceful and calm it is," Caroline murmured.

"And how stifling it will be within the church," Shirley replied. "What a tedious, long-winded speech Dr. Boultby will deliver! And how the curates will hammer away at their prepared orations! Personally, I would rather not enter."

"But my uncle will be furious if he notices our absence."

"I shall bear the brunt of his anger; he will not devour me. I am sorry to miss his sharp wit. I know his address will be all logic for the church and all stinging satire for the schismatics. He will not forget the Battle of Royd Lane. I am also sorry to deprive you of Mr. Hall's sincere, friendly homily, with all its zestful Yorkshire dialect; but here I must remain. The gray church and the grayer tombs look divine under this crimson light. Nature is now offering her evening prayers; she kneels before those red hills. I see her prostrate on the great steps of her altar, praying for a fair night for sailors at sea, for travelers in deserts, for lambs on the moors, and for unfledged birds in the woods. Caroline, I see her, and I will tell you what she resembles. She is what Eve was when she and Adam stood alone upon the earth."

"And that is not Milton's Eve, Shirley."

"Milton's Eve! Milton's Eve! I repeat. No, by the pure Mother of God, she is not! Cary, we are alone; we may speak our minds. Milton was great; but was he good? His intellect was correct; what of his heart? He saw heaven; he looked down upon hell. He beheld Satan, and Sin his daughter, and Death their horrific offspring. Serried angels stood before him in battalions; the long lines of adamantine shields flashed back upon his blind eyes the unutterable splendor of heaven. Devils gathered their legions in his sight; their dim, discrowned, tarnished armies passed rank and file before him. Milton attempted to see the first woman; but, Cary, he saw her not."

"You are bold to say so, Shirley."

"Not more bold than faithful. It was his cook he saw; or it was Mrs. Gill, as I have seen her, making custards in the heat of summer within the cool dairy, surrounded by rose-trees and nasturtiums near the latticed window, preparing a cold collation for the rectors—preserves and 'dulcet creams.' She puzzled over what choice to make for the finest delicacy, what arrangement would best avoid mixing tastes, not well-joined or inelegant, but bringing taste after taste, upheld with the kindest variety."

"All very well, Shirley."

"I would remind him that the first men of the earth were Titans, and that Eve was their mother; from her sprang Saturn, Hyperion, Oceanus; she bore Prometheus—"

"Pagan that you are! What does that signify?"

"I say, there were giants on the earth in those days—giants who strove to scale heaven. The first woman's breast, heaving with life on this world, yielded the daring to contend with Omnipotence, the strength to bear a thousand years of bondage, the vitality to feed that vulture of death through uncounted ages, the unexhausted life and uncorrupted excellence, sisters to immortality, which, after millennia of crimes, struggles, and woes, could conceive and bring forth a Messiah. The first woman was heaven-born. Vast was the heart from which gushed the wellspring of the blood of nations, and grand the undegenerate head where rested the consort-crown of creation."

"She coveted an apple and was cheated by a snake; but you have such a hash of Scripture and mythology in your head that no sense can be made of you. You have not yet told me what you saw kneeling on those hills."

"I saw—I now see—a Woman-Titan. Her robe of blue air spreads to the outskirts of the heath, where yonder flock grazes; a veil white as an avalanche sweeps from her head to her feet, with arabesques of lightning flame bordering it. Under her breast I see her zone, purple like that horizon; through its blush shines the star of evening. I cannot picture her steady eyes; they are clear, deep as lakes, lifted and full of worship, trembling with the softness of love and the lustre of prayer. Her forehead has the expanse of a cloud and is paler than the early moon, risen long before darkness gathers. She reclines her bosom on the ridge of Stilbro' Moor; her mighty hands are joined beneath it. So kneeling, face to face, she speaks with God. That Eve is Jehovah's daughter, as Adam was His son."

"She is very vague and visionary. Come, Shirley, we ought to go into church."

"Caroline, I will not; I will stay out here with my mother Eve, in these days called Nature. I love her—an undying, mighty being! Heaven may have faded from her brow when she fell in paradise, but all that is glorious on earth shines there still. She is taking me to her bosom, showing me her heart. Hush, Caroline! You will see her and feel as I do, if we are both silent."

"I will humour your whim; but you will begin talking again ere ten minutes are over."

Miss Keeldar, upon whom the soft excitement of the warm summer evening seemed to work with unwonted power, leaned against an upright headstone. She fixed her eyes on the deep-burning west and sank into a pleasurable trance. Caroline, moving a little apart, paced to and fro beneath the rectory garden wall, dreaming in her own way. Shirley had mentioned the word "mother." That word suggested to Caroline's imagination not the mighty and mystical parent of Shirley's visions, but a gentle human form—the shape she ascribed to her own mother, unknown, unloved, but not unlonged for.

"Oh that the day would come when she would remember her child! Oh that I might know her, and knowing, love her!"

Such was her aspiration.

The longing of her childhood filled her soul once more. The desire which many a night had kept her awake in her crib, and which fear of its fallacy had of late years almost extinguished, relit suddenly and glowed warm in her heart: that her mother might come some happy day, send for her to her presence, look upon her fondly with loving eyes, and say to her tenderly, in a sweet voice, "Caroline, my child, I have a home for you; you shall live with me. All the love you have needed, and not tasted, from infancy, I have saved for you carefully. Come; it shall cherish you now."

A noise on the road roused Caroline from her filial hopes and Shirley from her Titan visions. They listened and heard the tramp of horses. They looked and saw a glitter through the trees. Through the foliage, they caught glimpses of martial scarlet; a helm shone, a plume waved. Silent and orderly, six soldiers rode softly by.

"The same we saw this afternoon," whispered Shirley. "They have been halting somewhere until now. They wish to be as little noticed as possible and are seeking their rendezvous at this quiet hour, while the people are at church. Did I not say we should see unusual things ere long?"

Scarcely were the sight and sound of the soldiers lost when another, somewhat different disturbance broke the night-hush—a child's impatient scream. They looked. A man emerged from the church carrying in his arms an infant—a robust, ruddy little boy of some two years old—roaring with all the power of his lungs. He had probably just awakened from a church-sleep. Two little girls, aged nine and ten, followed. The influence of the fresh air and the attraction of some flowers gathered from a grave soon quieted the child. The man sat down with him, dandling him on his knee as tenderly as any woman; the two little girls took their places, one on each side.

"Good-evening, William," said Shirley, after a due scrutiny of the man. He had seen her before and appeared to be waiting to be recognized. He now took off his hat and grinned a smile of pleasure. He was a rough-headed, hard-featured personage, not old but very weather-beaten. His attire was decent and clean; that of his children was singularly neat. It was our old friend Farren. The young ladies approached him.

"You are not going into the church?" he inquired, gazing at them complacently, yet with a mixture of bashfulness in his look—a sentiment not by any means the result of awe of their station, but only of appreciation of their elegance and youth. Before gentlemen—such as Moore or Helstone, for instance—William was often a little dogged; with proud or insolent ladies, too, he was quite unmanageable, sometimes very resentful; but he was most sensible of, most tractable to, good-humor and civility. His nature—a stubborn one—was repelled by inflexibility in other natures; for which reason he had never been able to like his former master, Moore; and unconscious of that gentleman's good opinion of himself, and of the service he had secretly rendered him in recommending him as gardener to Mr. Yorke, and by this means to other families in the neighborhood, he continued to harbor a grudge against his austerity. Latterly he had often worked at Fieldhead. Miss Keeldar's frank, hospitable manners were perfectly charming to him. Caroline he had known from her childhood; unconsciously she was his ideal of a lady. Her gentle mien, step, gestures, her grace of person and attire, moved some artist-fibers about his peasant heart. He had a pleasure in looking at her, as he had in examining rare flowers or in seeing pleasant landscapes. Both the ladies liked William; it was their delight to lend him books, to give him plants; and they preferred his conversation far before that of many coarse, hard, pretentious people immeasurably higher in station.

"Who was speaking, William, when you came out?" asked Shirley.

"A gentleman ye set a deal of store on, Miss Shirley—Mr. Donne."

"You look knowing, William. How did you find out my regard for Mr. Donne?"

"Ay, Miss Shirley, there's a gleg light i' your een sometimes which betrays you. You look raight down scornful sometimes when Mr. Donne is by."

"Do you like him yourself, William?"

"Me? I'm stalled o' t' curates, and so is t' wife. They've no manners. They talk to poor folk fair as if they thought they were beneath them. They're allus magnifying their office. It is a pity but their office could magnify them; but it does nought o' t' soart. I fair hate pride."

"But you are proud in your own way yourself," interposed Caroline. "You are what you call house-proud: you like to have everything handsome about you. Sometimes you look as if you were almost too proud to take your wages. When you were out of work, you were too proud to get anything on credit. But for your children, I believe you would rather have starved than gone to the shops without money; and when I wanted to give you something, what a difficulty I had in making you take it!"

"It is partly true, Miss Caroline. Ony day I'd rather give than take, especially from sich as ye. Look at t' difference between us. Ye're a little, young, slender lass, and I'm a great strong man; I'm rather more nor twice your age. It is not _my_ part, then, I think, to tak fro' _ye_--to be under obligations (as they say) to _ye_. And that day ye came to our house, and called me to t' door, and offered me five shillings, which I doubt ye could ill spare--for ye've no fortin', I know--that day I war fair a rebel, a radical, an insurrectionist; and _ye_ made me so. I thought it shameful that, willing and able as I was to work, I suld be i' such a condition that a young cratur about the age o' my own eldest lass suld think it needful to come and offer me her bit o' brass."

"I suppose you were angry with me, William?"

"I almost was, in a way. But I forgave ye varry soon. Ye meant well. Ay, _I am_ proud, and so are _ye_; but your pride and mine is t' raight mak--what we call i' Yorkshire clean pride--such as Mr. Malone and Mr. Donne knows nought about. Theirs is mucky pride. Now, I shall teach my lasses to be as proud as Miss Shirley there, and my lads to be as proud as myseln; but I dare ony o' 'em to be like t' curates. I'd lick little Michael if I seed him show any signs o' that feeling."

"What is the difference, William?"

"Ye know t' difference weel enow, but ye want me to get a gate o' talking. Mr. Malone and Mr. Donne is almost too proud to do aught for theirseln; _we_ are almost too proud to let anybody do aught for us. T' curates can hardly bide to speak a civil word to them they think beneath them; _we_ can hardly bide to tak an uncivil word fro' them that thinks themseln aboon us."

"Now, William, be humble enough to tell me truly how you are getting on in the world.

The twilight deepened, painting the sky in hues of bruised purple and gold, a canvas perfectly suited for the kindling of stars. At the rectory garden gate, Miss Keeldar adjusted her shawl, her voice light despite the gathering shadows. "There will be just light enough to show me the way home," she said, preparing to depart.

"You must not go alone, Shirley; Fanny shall accompany you."

"That she shall not," Shirley replied with a decisive shake of her head. "Of what need should I be afraid in my own parish? I would walk from Fieldhead to the church on any fine midsummer night, three hours later than this, for the mere pleasure of seeing the stars and the chance of meeting a fairy."

"But wait until the crowd disperses."

"Agreed," Shirley smiled, spotting the procession of carriages and phaetons arriving—the Armitage sisters, Mrs. Sykes, Mr. Wynne. "I have no wish to perform the ceremony of bidding them all good-bye. Let us slip into the garden and take shelter amongst the laburnums for an instant."

As the rectors, curates, and churchwardens emerged from the porch, engaging in a flurry of handshakes and congratulations on the evening's success, the throng gradually dissolved. Carriages rattled away, leaving the garden quiet once more. Just as Shirley stepped out from her floral refuge, Mr. Helstone entered, his face illuminated by a sudden urgency.

"Oh, I want you!" he exclaimed, catching her before she could retreat. "I was afraid you were already gone. Caroline, come here."

Caroline approached, expecting a lecture on her invisibility during the service, but the rector's mind was occupied with other matters. "I shall not sleep at home tonight," he continued. "I have met an old friend and promised to accompany him. I expect to return about noon tomorrow. Thomas, the clerk, is engaged, and I cannot arrange for him to sleep in the house as I usually do when I am away."

"Now," Shirley interrupted, her eyes flashing with a sudden, sharp intelligence, "you want me as a gentleman—the first gentleman in Briarfield, in short—to supply your place, be master of the rectory, and guardian of your niece and maids while you are away?"

"Exactly, Captain. I thought the post would suit you. Will you favor Caroline so far as to be her guest for one night? Will you stay here instead of returning to Fieldhead?"

"And what will Mrs. Pryor do? She expects me home."

"I will send her word. Come, make up your mind to stay. It grows late; the dew falls heavily. You and Caroline will enjoy each other's company, I doubt not."

"I promise you, then, to stay with Caroline," Shirley replied firmly. "As you say, we shall enjoy each other's society. We will not be separated tonight. Now, rejoin your old friend, and fear nothing for us."

"If there should chance to be any disturbance in the night, Captain," Mr. Helstone warned, his tone shifting to something more serious, "if you hear the picking of a lock, the cutting of a pane of glass, or a stealthy tread of steps about the house—and I need not fear to tell *you*, who bear a well-tempered, mettlesome heart under your girl's ribbon sash, that such incidents are very possible in the present time—what would you do?"

"Don't know; faint, perhaps—fall down and have to be picked up again." Shirley laughed lightly. "But, Doctor, if you assign me the post of honor, you must give me arms. What weapons are there in your stronghold?"

"You could not wield a sword?"

"No; I could manage the carving-knife better."

"You will find a good one in the dining-room sideboard—a lady's knife, light to handle, and as sharp-pointed as a poniard."

"It will suit Caroline. But you must give me a brace of pistols. I know you have pistols."

"I have two pairs. One pair I can place at your disposal. You will find them suspended over the mantelpiece of my study in cloth cases."

"Loaded?"

"Yes, but not on the cock. Cock them before you go to bed. It is paying you a great compliment, Captain, to lend you these. Were you one of the awkward squad, you should not have them."

"I will take care. You need delay no longer, Mr. Helstone. You may go now." As the rector passed out at the garden gate, Shirley murmured, "He is gracious to me to lend me his pistols." Turning to Caroline, she added, "Come, Lina, let us go in and have some supper. I was too much vexed at tea with the vicinage of Mr. Sam Wynne to be able to eat, and now I am really hungry."

Entering the house, they repaired to the darkened dining-room. Through the open windows, the evening air drifted in, carrying the perfume of flowers from the garden, the distant sound of receding footsteps from the road, and a soft, vague murmur. Caroline stood at the casement, listening intently. "Shirley, I hear the beck in the Hollow," she whispered.

She rang the bell, requesting a candle, bread, and milk—Miss Keeldar's usual supper. When Fanny brought the tray, she hesitated to close the windows and shutters, but Shirley requested she desist. The twilight was too calm, its breath too balmy to be excluded. They took their meal in silence. Caroline rose once to remove a glass of flowers from the sideboard, the scent too powerful for the sultry room. In returning, she half-opened a drawer and took out something that glittered clear and keen in her hand.

"You assigned this to me, then, Shirley, did you?" Caroline asked, holding the long knife. "It is bright, keen-edged, finely tapered; it is dangerous-looking. I never yet felt the impulse which could move me to direct this against a fellow-creature. It is difficult to fancy that circumstances could nerve my arm to strike home with this long knife."

"I should hate to do it," Shirley replied, "but I think I could do it, if goaded by certain exigencies which I can imagine." She quietly sipped her glass of new milk, looking somewhat thoughtful and a little pale—a complexion she rarely lacked.

After the milk and bread were consumed, Fanny was summoned again. She and Eliza were sent to bed, weary from the day's exertions of cutting currant-buns and filling urns. Soon the maids' chamber door closed. Caroline took a candle and quietly inspected the house, ensuring every window was fast and every door barred. She did not even evade the haunted back kitchen nor the vault-like cellars.

"There is neither spirit nor flesh in the house at present," she said, returning to the dining-room. "It is now near eleven o'clock, fully bedtime; yet I would rather sit up a little longer, if you do not object, Shirley." She placed the brace of pistols from her uncle's study on the table.

"Why would you rather sit up longer?" Shirley asked, taking up the firearms and examining them.

"Because I have a strange, excited feeling in my heart."

"So have I."

"Is this state of sleeplessness and restlessness caused by something electrical in the air, I wonder?"

"No; the sky is clear, the stars numberless. It is a fine night."

"But very still. I hear the water fret over its stony bed in Hollow's Copse as distinctly as if it ran below the churchyard wall."

"I am glad it is so still a night. A moaning wind or rushing rain would vex me to fever just now."

"Why, Shirley?"

"Because it would baffle my efforts to listen."

"Do you listen towards the Hollow?"

"Yes; it is the only quarter whence we can hear a sound just now."

They both sat near the window, leaning their arms on the sill, inclining their heads towards the open lattice. By the starlight and the dim June twilight, they saw each other's young faces clearly.

"Mr. Helstone thinks we have no idea which way he is gone," Shirley murmured, "nor on what errand, nor with what expectations, nor how prepared. But I guess much, do not you?"

"I guess something."

"All those gentlemen—your cousin Moore included—think that you and I are now asleep in our beds, unconscious."

"Caring nothing about them—hoping and fearing nothing for them," Caroline added.

Silence stretched between them for half an hour. The night was silent too, measured only by the church clock's quarters. They wrapped their scarves closer, resumed their bonnets, and watched.

Towards midnight, the teasing, monotonous bark of the house-dog disturbed the quietude. Caroline rose noiselessly to the kitchen to appease him with bread. Returning to the dining-room, she found it dark; Shirley had extinguished the candle. The outline of her shape was visible near the still-open window, leaning out. Caroline stole to her side. The dog recommenced barking furiously, then suddenly stopped, seeming to listen. The occupants of the dining-room listened too, not merely to the flow of the mill-stream, but to a nearer, though muffled, sound on the road below the churchyard—a measured, beating, approaching sound, a dull tramp of marching feet.

It drew near. Those who listened began to comprehend its extent. It was not the tread of two, nor a dozen, nor a score; it was the tread of hundreds. They could see nothing; the high shrubs of the garden formed a leafy screen between them and the road. To hear, however, was not enough. As the troop trod forward, seeming to pass the rectory, they felt it more when a human voice broke the hush of the night.

"Halt!"

A halt followed. The march was arrested. Then came a low conference, indistinguishable from the dining-room.

"We *must* hear this," Shirley said.

She turned, took her pistols from the table, silently passed out through the middle window—which was a glass door—and stole down the walk to the garden wall, standing listening under the lilacs. Caroline would not have quitted the house had she been alone, but where Shirley went, she would go. She glanced at the weapon on the sideboard but left it behind, standing at her friend's side. They dared not look over the wall, for fear of being seen; they were obliged to crouch behind it.

"It looks a rambling old building. Who lives in it besides the damned parson?"

"Only three women—his niece and two servants."

"Do you know where they sleep?"

"The lasses behind; the niece in a front room."

"And Helstone?"

"Yonder is his chamber. He was burning a light, but I see none now."

"Where would you get in?"

"If I were ordered to do his job—and he deserves it—I'd try yond' long window; it opens to the dining-room. I could grope my way upstairs, and I know his chamber."

"How would you manage about the women folk?"

"Let 'em alone except they shrieked, and then I'd soon quieten 'em. I could wish to find the old chap asleep. If he waked, he'd be dangerous."

"Has he arms?"

"Firearms, allus—and allus loadened."

"Then you're a fool to stop us here. A shot would give the alarm. Moore would be on us before we could turn round. We should miss our main object."

"You might go on, I tell you. I'd engage Helstone alone."

A pause. One of the party dropped some weapon, which rang on the stone causeway. At this sound, the rectory dog barked again fiercely.

"That spoils all!" said the voice. "He'll awake. A noise like that might rouse the dead. You did not say there was a dog. Damn you! Forward!"

Forward they went—tramp, tramp—with mustering, manifold, slow-filing tread. They were gone.

Shirley stood erect, looked over the wall, along the road. "Not a soul remains," she said.

She stood and mused. "Thank God!" was the next observation. Caroline repeated the ejaculation, not in so steady a tone. She was trembling much. Her heart beat fast and thick; her face was cold, her forehead damp.

"Thank God for us!" she reiterated. "But what will happen elsewhere? They have passed us by that they may make sure of others."

"They have done well," returned Shirley, with composure. "The others will defend themselves. They can do it. They are prepared for them. With us it is otherwise. My finger was on the trigger of this pistol. I was quite ready to give that man, if he had entered, such a greeting as he little calculated on; but behind him followed three hundred. I had neither three hundred hands nor three hundred weapons. I could not have effectually protected either you, myself, or the two poor women asleep under that roof. Therefore I again earnestly thank God for insult and peril escaped."

After a second pause, she continued: "What is it my duty and wisdom to do next? Not to stay here inactive, I am glad to say, but, of course, to walk over to the Hollow."

"To the Hollow, Shirley?"

"To the Hollow. Will you go with me?"

"Where those men are gone?"

"They have taken the highway; we should not encounter them. The road over the fields is as safe, silent, and solitary as a path through the air would be. Will you go?"

"Yes," was the answer, given mechanically, not because the speaker wished or was prepared to go, or indeed was otherwise than scared at the prospect, but because she felt she could not abandon Shirley.

"Then we must fasten up these windows, and leave all as secure as we can behind us. Do you know what we are going for, Cary?"

"Yes—no—because you wish it."

"Is that all? And are you so obedient to a mere caprice of mine? What a docile wife you would make to a

The road back to the rectory was empty; the two girls found no living soul to greet them. They slipped inside without a sound, ascending the stairs as silently as shadows, the pale grey light of the breaking dawn providing just enough illumination for their hurried retreat. Shirley threw herself onto her couch immediately. Though the room was unfamiliar—she had never slept in the rectory before—and though the events of the night were a spectacle of terror and excitement unlike anything she had ever witnessed, the moment her head touched the pillow, a deep, restorative sleep claimed her. It silenced her senses and smoothed her brow.

Perfect health was Shirley's fortune. Warm-hearted and sympathetic though she was, she possessed no nervous constitution; powerful emotions could stir her without draining her spirit. The storm had shaken her while it raged, but it had left her resilience unbroken and her freshness untouched. As each day brought her stimulating emotion, so each night yielded her renewing rest. Caroline watched her sleeping form, reading the profound serenity of Shirley's mind in the beauty of her peaceful face.

For herself, being of a different temperament, Caroline could not find rest. The mere commonplace excitement of the tea-drinking and the gathering at the school would have been enough to keep her awake; the effect of the terrible drama that had just unfolded before her eyes was unlikely to fade for days. It was futile even to try to lie down; she remained seated by Shirley's side, counting the slow minutes and watching the June sun climb higher in the heavens.

Life wasted quickly in such vigils as Caroline had kept of late—watching hours where the mind, lacking pleasant nourishment, no manna of hope, and no honey of joyful memories, tried to survive on the meagre diet of wishes. Finding neither delight nor support in these empty cravings, and feeling herself ready to perish from want, she turned to philosophy, to resolution, to resignation. She called upon these gods for aid, but they remained unheard, unhelped, leaving her to languish.
